(27)

The source globes satisfy horizontal composition laws given by the group law. Since
\gamma(g)
satisfy the group law as well, this defines a map of
\infty
-groupoids by extending to the identity on all higher globes, yielding the required homotopy.
∎

2.3
Stabilization and an
\Omega
-spectrum

In this section we extend the definition of QCA representation and blend anomaly to study obstructions to stable disentangling. In doing so, we construct an
\Omega
-spectrum associated to QCA with a fixed algebra of single-site operators. As well as an interesting result in its own right, it allows many technical simplifications in the question of the homotopy class of a QCA representation. For example it implies that these homotopy classes, ie. the “stable” blend anomalies, form an abelian group.

To motivate the definition, suppose we consider a blend from the identity to the identity along the 1st axis,
\beta:1\equiv_{1}1
. Such a blend is a QCA
\beta
which acts as the identity on operators supported outside of the blending region

\begin{gathered}I\times{\mathbb{Z}}^{d-1}\subset{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}.\end{gathered}

(28)

If the spread of
\beta
is
s
, then it is simple to show that
\beta
preserves the subalgebra
{\mathcal{A}}(I^{+s}\times{\mathbb{Z}}^{d-1})
of operators supported in

\begin{gathered}I^{+s}\times{\mathbb{Z}}^{d-1},\end{gathered}

(29)

where
I^{+s}
is
\{x\in{\mathbb{Z}}\ |\ \text{dist}(x,I)\leq s\}
. Furthermore,
\beta
is determined by its action on
{\mathcal{A}}(I^{+s}\times{\mathbb{Z}}^{d-1})
. This is a local algebra on
{\mathbb{Z}}^{d-1}
since the finite factor
I^{+s}
can be compressed into a single site of
{\mathbb{Z}}^{d-1}
, growing its dimension by a finite amount
15
15

15

It is dangerous to consider infinite compressions of this sort, as the site Hilbert spaces would become uncountably infinite dimensional and are in fact
not
Hilbert spaces at all, which is a classic difficulty in many-body quantum mechanics in infinite volume
51
. Countably infinite dimensional site Hilbert spaces are admissible in our definitions, however.
. Thus,
\beta
is equivalent to a QCA on
{\mathcal{A}}(I^{+s}\times{\mathbb{Z}}^{d-1})
.

When we compose blends from the identity to itself, they behave almost as
d-1
-dimensional QCA, except that the blend interval
I
in general will grow with each composition. This motivates the following definition.

Definition 10
.

Let
{\mathcal{H}}
be a Hilbert space which will be the Hilbert space associated with each site of our lattice. Call this the
site Hilbert space
. We will assume this Hilbert space is finite-dimensional, although this and many definitions extend to general Hilbert spaces. For fermions we use a super Hilbert space.

We define the local algebra
{\mathcal{A}}^{\omega}_{\mathcal{H}}
on the infinite-dimensional lattice
\mathbb{Z}^{\omega}
with axes ordered by positive integers
1,2,3,\dots
in the usual way: for each region
R\subseteq{\mathbb{Z}}^{\omega}
,
{\mathcal{A}}^{\omega}_{\mathcal{H}}(R)=\bigotimes_{\vec{x}\in R}M({\mathcal{H}})
is the algebra of operators whose support is contained in
R
, where
M({\mathcal{H}})
the
\star
-algebra of endomorphisms of
{\mathcal{H}}
(typically finite matrices). We embed
{\mathbb{Z}}^{\omega}
in
\mathbb{R}^{\omega}
equipped with any product metric, and we consider QCA on this algebra.

A
stable
d
-QCA
on
{\mathcal{A}}^{\omega}_{\mathcal{H}}
is a QCA on
{\mathcal{A}}^{\omega}_{\mathcal{H}}
such that there exist an integer
l
and and interval
[a,b]
which define the
domain of
\alpha
:

\begin{gathered}D(\alpha)=\left\{(x_{1},\ldots)\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{\omega}\ \Bigg|\ \begin{cases}x_{i}\text{ unconstrained}&1\leq i\leq d\\
a\leq x_{i}\leq b&d<i\leq d+l\\
x_{i}=0&i>d+l\end{cases}\right\}\\
={\mathbb{Z}}^{d}\times[a,b]^{l}\times 0\times 0\times\cdots\end{gathered}

(30)

such that

1.

\alpha
preserves the subalgebra
{\mathcal{A}}^{\omega}_{\mathcal{H}}(D(\alpha))
of operators supported in
D(\alpha)
.

2.

\alpha
acts by the identity on operators supported outside
D(\alpha)
.

\alpha
is thus determined by a QCA on
{\mathcal{A}}^{\omega}_{\mathcal{H}}(D(\alpha))
. This is illustrated in Fig.
4
.

A
stable local unitary
is an equivalence class of an integer
l
and an interval
[a,b]
and a local unitary operator acting on the (finite dimensional) Hilbert space

\begin{gathered}\bigotimes_{\vec{x}\in[a,b]^{l}}{\mathcal{H}},\end{gathered}

(31)

which is equivalent to its extension by the identity under inclusions
[a,b]^{l}\subset[a^{\prime},b^{\prime}]^{l^{\prime}}
for
l^{\prime}\geq l
,
a^{\prime}\leq a
, and
b^{\prime}\geq b
. In other words, the group of stable local unitaries is the direct limit of the unitary groups
U([a,b]^{l})
under these inclusions. Stable local unitaries also define stable 0-QCA on
{\mathbb{Z}}^{\omega}
with domain
[a,b]^{l}
by the adjoint action of these unitaries.

Given two stable
d
-QCA, with
l_{1},a_{1},b_{1}
and
l_{2},a_{2},b_{2}
, we can always consider them on their “common domain” taking
l=\max(l_{1},l_{2})
,
a=\min(a_{1},a_{2})
,
b=\max(b_{1},b_{2})
, by extending by the identity. They can then be composed on their common domain to give a stable
d
-QCA. Composition is associative and invertible, with the inverse of a stable
d
-QCA given by the QCA
\alpha^{-1}
on the same domain.

A
blend
\gamma:\alpha\equiv_{i}\beta
between stable
d
-QCA
is a stable
d
-QCA whose domain contains the common domain of
\alpha
and
\beta
, and equals
\alpha
(resp.
\beta
) to the left (right) of a blending interval along the
i
th axis.

All of this gives enough structure to define an
\infty
-groupoid of stable
d
-QCA

\mathcal{Q}^{d}_{\mathcal{H}}
. This
\infty
-groupoid is defined by taking
k+2
-globes to be blends along the
d-k
th axis for
0\leq k\leq d-1
and
d+2
-globes to be stable local unitaries. See
Section
4
for more details.

Figure 4:
The domain
D(\alpha)
of a stable
d
-QCA
\alpha
is illustrated.
\alpha
is required to preserve the subalgebra of operators supported in
D(\alpha)
and act by the identity on all operators supported outside
D(\alpha)
. It is thus determined by a QCA on the “thickened”
d
-dimensional space
D(\alpha)
. This allows us to freely utilize ancillas and is crucial for obtaining an
\Omega
spectrum of QCA.

Consider the local algebra
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathcal{H}}^{d}
on
{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}
with site Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}}
. We can consider any QCA
\alpha
on
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathcal{H}}^{d}
to be a stable
d
-QCA with the domain (
l=0
)

\begin{gathered}D(\alpha)={\mathbb{Z}}^{d}\times 0\times 0\times\cdots.\end{gathered}

(32)

This defines a map of
\infty
-groupoids

\begin{gathered}{\mathcal{Q}}({\mathcal{A}}_{\mathcal{H}}^{d})\to{\mathcal{Q}}^{d},\end{gathered}

(33)

which can be thought of as a “stabilization”, allowing the addition of ancillas to the study of QCA.
In particular we have the following easy corollary of the definition (see
Proposition
15
):

Theorem 3
.

The
\infty
-groupoid
{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{\mathcal{H}}
of stable
d
-QCA with site Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}}
has fundamental group (for
d\geq 1
)

\begin{gathered}\pi_{1}({\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{\mathcal{H}})=\{(\alpha,n)\ |\ n\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1},\ \alpha\text{ is a QCA on }{\mathcal{A}}^{d}_{{\mathcal{H}}^{\otimes n}}\}/\sim\end{gathered}

(34)

where
\sim
is the equivalence relation generated by the two basic equivalences

1.

(\alpha,n)\sim(\beta,n)
if there is a blend along the
d
th axis from
\alpha
to
\beta
as QCA on
{\mathcal{A}}^{d}_{{\mathcal{H}}^{\otimes n}}

2.

(\alpha,n)\sim(\alpha\otimes 1,n+m)
where
\alpha\otimes 1
is the QCA on
{\mathcal{A}}^{d}_{{\mathcal{H}}^{\otimes(n+m)}}={\mathcal{A}}^{d}_{{\mathcal{H}}^{\otimes n}}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}^{d}_{{\mathcal{H}}^{\otimes m}}
which acts as
\alpha
on the first factor and the identity on the second.

Let
[\alpha,n]
denote the equivalence classes. The (abelian) group structure on
\pi_{1}({\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{\mathcal{H}})
is given by

\begin{gathered}+[\beta,m]=[\alpha\otimes\beta,n+m]\end{gathered}

(35)

which is also equal to

\begin{gathered}+[\beta,n]=[\alpha\circ\beta,n].\end{gathered}

(36)

We have

\begin{gathered}=[\alpha^{-1},n]=-[\alpha,n],\end{gathered}

(37)

where
\alpha^{\text{rev}}
is the reversal of
\alpha
, obtained by
\alpha^{\text{rev}}=R\circ\alpha\circ R^{-1}
, where
R
is any permutation of sites, acting on
{\mathcal{A}}^{d}_{{\mathcal{H}}^{\otimes n}}
by some reflection over the
d
th axis (
R
is not a QCA but
R\circ\alpha\circ R^{-1}
is).

We likewise define a notion of stable QCA symmetry.

Definition 11
.

A
stable
d
-QCA
G
-representation (with site Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
)
is a collection of stable
d
-QCA
\{\alpha(g)\}_{g\in G}
with site Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
satisfying the
G
group law under composition:

\begin{gathered}\alpha(g)\alpha(h)=\alpha(gh).\end{gathered}

(38)

These definitions allow the easy inclusion of ancillas using the extra directions, allowing us also to characterize stably disentanglable
G
-representations. Indeed, a QCA
G
-representation
\alpha
on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
with site Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
defines a stable
d
-QCA
G
-representation, and therefore a map

\begin{gathered}\alpha:BG\to\mathcal{Q}^{d}_{\mathcal{H}}.\end{gathered}

(39)

As before, we find that if
\alpha
is stably-disentanglable, then
\alpha
is homotopic to the constant map (see
Proposition
4
below). This motivates the following definition.

Definition 12
.

The
stable blend anomaly
of a QCA
G
-representation
\alpha
on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
with site Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
is the (based
16
16

16

Below we will show
{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{\mathcal{H}}
has an
H
-space structure, being a component of a loop space. Therefore, it does not matter if we consider homotopies of based or unbased maps. See Proposition 1.4.3 of
48
.
) homotopy class
[\alpha]
of the induced map
\alpha:BG\to\mathcal{Q}^{d}_{\mathcal{H}}
.

Theorem 1
now follows easily from the following proposition.

Proposition 4
.

Let
{\mathcal{H}}
be a Hilbert space. Suppose
\alpha
is a QCA
G
-representation on the local algebra
{\mathcal{A}}^{d}_{\mathcal{H}}
on
{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}
with site Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}}
. Suppose further that
\alpha
is stably disentanglable upon introducing ancillas in
{\mathcal{H}}
(a finite number per site). Then the associated map

\begin{gathered}\alpha:BG\to{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{\mathcal{H}}\end{gathered}

(40)

is null-homotopic, i.e. the stable blend anomaly
[\alpha]
vanishes and
Theorem
1
holds.

Proof.

The introduction of
n
ancillas per site amounts to considering the enlargement

\begin{gathered}\alpha^{\prime}(g)=\alpha(g)\otimes 1\end{gathered}

(41)

acting in
{\mathcal{A}}^{d}_{{\mathcal{H}}^{\otimes(n+1)}}={\mathcal{A}}^{d}_{\mathcal{H}}\otimes({\mathcal{A}}^{d}_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{\otimes n}
. Considered as stable
d
-QCA,
\alpha(g)
, acting in the domain

\begin{gathered}{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}\times\{0\}\times\cdots\end{gathered}

(42)

and
\alpha^{\prime}(g)
, acting in the domain

\begin{gathered}{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}\times\{0,1,\ldots,n\}\times\{0\}\times\cdots,\end{gathered}

(43)

which extends
\alpha(g)
by the identity, these stable
d
-QCA are equal. Thus, if
\alpha^{\prime}
is disentanglable, the same argument as in
Proposition
2
constructs a set of stable blends
\alpha(g)\equiv_{1}1
satisfying the group law, and this yields a nullhomotopy of
\alpha:BG\to{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{\mathcal{H}}
by
Lemma
1
(this lemma has the same proof in the stable case).
∎

There is another fortuitous consequence of this definition. In particular, we find that a stable blend of
d
-QCA along the
d
th axis from the identity to itself is the same thing as a stable
d-1
-QCA. Consider the space
\Omega_{\star}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{\mathcal{H}}
of loops beginning and ending at the distinguished point
\star
(the unique object of
{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{\mathcal{H}}
as we have constructed it). This space itself is an
\infty
-groupoid whose objects are endomorphisms
{\rm Hom}(\star,\star)
, ie. stable
d
-QCAs, and whose 1-morphisms are 2-morphisms, and so on. Thus
\Omega_{\star}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}
is a space of QCA. If we consider loops in this space based at the identity, we get another space
\Omega_{\rm id}\Omega_{\star}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{\mathcal{H}}
. From the globular presentation, this space is precisely
\Omega_{\star}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d-1}_{\mathcal{H}}
:

Theorem 5
.

The space
\Omega_{\star}\mathcal{Q}^{d}_{\mathcal{H}}
of stable
d
-QCA for a fixed site Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
satisfies

\begin{gathered}\Omega_{\rm id}\Omega_{\star}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{\mathcal{H}}\cong\Omega_{\star}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d-1}_{\mathcal{H}}.\end{gathered}

(44)

In other words,
\Omega_{\star}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{\mathcal{H}}
defines an
\Omega
-spectrum.

This means in particular that the homotopy classes of maps from a space
X
to
\mathcal{Q}^{d}
satisfies the axioms of a (reduced) generalized cohomology theory
\mathcal{Q}^{d}(X)
. In particular, they form an abelian group, and the stable blend anomaly is an element of the group
\mathcal{Q}^{d}(BG)
. This is closely related to the
\Omega
-spectrum conjecture for invertible gapped phases, a topic we return to in
Section
3.1
.

We find the following:

Corollary 1
.

The stable blend anomaly for a stable
d
-dimensional QCA
G
-representation
\alpha
is an element of an abelian group

\begin{gathered}\in{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{\mathcal{H}}(BG).\end{gathered}

(45)

If we have a second such representation
\beta
, and consider the tensor product representation
[\alpha\otimes\beta]
, then

\begin{gathered}=[\alpha]+[\beta].\end{gathered}

(46)

Let
\alpha^{\rm rev}
be
\alpha
reflected anywhere along the first axis. Then

\begin{gathered}=-[\alpha].\end{gathered}

(47)

Proof.

The additive structure of the maps is abstractly defined by

\begin{gathered}(\alpha+\beta):BG\xrightarrow{\Delta}BG\times BG\xrightarrow{(\alpha,\beta)}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}\times{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}\xrightarrow{*}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d},\end{gathered}

(48)

where
\Delta
is the diagonal map, and
*
is the product on
{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}
, which comes from
{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}
being equivalent to the full subcategory of the identity object (constant loop) in
\Omega_{\star}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d+1}
, or equivalently
B\Omega_{id}\Omega_{\star}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d+1}
. Since
\Omega_{\star}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d+1}
is an infinite loop space (it is
\Omega_{\rm id}^{n}\Omega_{\star}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d+n}_{\mathcal{H}}
for all
n\geq 0
), this product gives an abelian group structure to the homotopy classes of maps
BG\to{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}
, since loops may be concatenated
49
.

To be more concrete, on the 1-morphism level the product on
{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}
is given by the composition of blends
1\equiv_{1}1
, which is stable
d
-QCA composition. So the map
(\alpha+\beta)
sends the 1-morphism
\star\xrightarrow{g}\star
of
BG
to
\alpha(g)\beta(g)
.

In general,
\alpha(g)\beta(g)
is
not
a QCA
G
-representation unless
\alpha(g)
and
\beta(h)
commute for all
g,h\in G
. Indeed, in this case the higher morphisms of
BG
will activate non-trivial QCA with the combinatorial structure of the diagonal map
\Delta
encoding the non-trivial commutators. So although
\alpha(g)\beta(g)
is not a QCA
G
-representation, this higher data makes it a QCA
G
-representation
up to (coherent) homotopy
.

In the special case that all the commutators are trivial,
\alpha(g)\beta(g)
is a QCA
G
-representation, and all the higher data collapses to yield identity morphisms. This occurs for example if
\alpha
and
\beta
have disjoint domains. In this case, the operation of composition is the same as stacking, and we have
(\alpha+\beta)=\alpha\otimes\beta
.

We can in fact always choose a homotopy representative
[\alpha^{\prime}]=[\alpha]
such that
\alpha^{\prime}
and
\beta
have disjoint domains, by a “layer shifting homotopy” as shown in Fig.
5
. This produces blends of
G
-representations which themselves are
G
-representations, so by
Lemma
1
, we get homotopies. Since the homotopy class
[\alpha+\beta]
only depends on the homotopy classes
[\alpha]
,
[\beta]
,

\begin{gathered}=[\alpha^{\prime}+\beta]=[\alpha^{\prime}\beta]=[\alpha\otimes\beta].\end{gathered}

(49)

To show
[\alpha^{\text{rev}}]=-[\alpha]
, we show
[\alpha]+[\alpha^{\text{rev}}]=0
. For this, we observe that we can blend
\alpha\otimes\alpha^{\text{rev}}
to the identity representation through blends which are
G
-representations by folding, as in Fig.
6
. The result then follows from
Lemma
1
.

∎

Figure 5:
A “layer shifting” blend of
G
-representations along the first axis (other axes not shown), may be constructed by conjugating a QCA
G
-representation by layer swaps between layer
0
and
1
on a half-space of
{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}
. The result is shown. By construction, these blends satisfy the group law. By
Lemma
1
, this defines a homotopy of the corresponding maps
BG\to{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{\mathcal{H}}
.

Figure 6:
A “folding” blend from the QCA
G
-representation
\alpha(g)\otimes\alpha^{\text{rev}}(g)
to the identity, which again may be constructed by a suitable permutation of sites. By
Lemma
1
, this defines a homotopy of the corresponding maps
BG\to{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{\mathcal{H}}
.

From now on, we will mostly focus on stable anomalies, and say “unstable” when referring to the non-stabilized version built on
{\mathcal{Q}}({\mathcal{A}})
.

2.4
Anomaly Indices and the Else-Nayak Index

This abstract structure is very satisfying, but it leaves us with a practical problem. Given a QCA
G
-representation, how do we compute its blend anomaly?

From the homotopy point of view, we want to ask whether the map

\begin{gathered}\alpha:BG\to{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{\mathcal{H}}\end{gathered}

(50)

(or the unstable version) is null-homotopic. There is a method in algebraic topology known as obstruction theory which allows us to answer questions like this. We will present the version based on a “homotopy lifting problem”. This version is simpler to reason about abstractly, although for computations in
Section
4.2
we will also discuss the equivalent “homotopy extension problem”
65
;
32
;
49
, which more closely resembles the construction of Else and Nayak
16
.

We begin by introducing the “Whitehead tower” of
{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{\mathcal{H}}
, which is a sequence of fibrations (in
\infty
-groupoids
24
)

\begin{gathered}\cdots\to{\mathcal{Q}}^{d,2}_{\mathcal{H}}\xrightarrow{p_{2}}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d,1}_{\mathcal{H}}\xrightarrow{p_{1}}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d,0}_{\mathcal{H}}={\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{\mathcal{H}}.\end{gathered}

(51)

The first fibration is the universal cover, and the next are generalizations of these, which we write as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{k-1}\pi_{k}({\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{\mathcal{H}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Q}}^{d,k}_{\mathcal{H}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Q}}^{d,k-1}_{\mathcal{H}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{k}\pi_{k}({\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{\mathcal{H}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c^{k-1}}

(52)

where
B^{k-1}
is the
k-1
-fold delooping of
\pi_{k}({\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{\mathcal{H}})
and
c^{k-1}
is the classifying map of this fibration
24
. In particular,
{\mathcal{Q}}^{d,k}_{\mathcal{H}}
is
k
-connected, ie.
\pi_{\leq k}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d,k}_{\mathcal{H}}=0
. It is a special property of
{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{\mathcal{H}}
that this tower tops out in finitely many steps, with

\begin{gathered}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d,d+2}_{\mathcal{H}}\cong B^{d+2}U(1)_{disc}\\
{\mathcal{Q}}^{d,\geq d+3}_{\mathcal{H}}\cong\star,\end{gathered}

(53)

where
U(1)_{disc}
indicates
U(1)
with the discrete topology
17
17

17

It is possible to consider
\infty
-groupoids enriched in topological spaces, by giving a non-trivial topology to the set of globes. There is a natural topology on stable local unitaries which replaces the
U(1)_{disc}
at the top with a
{\mathbb{Z}}
one step higher. It is likely necessary to consider this topology when studying anomalies of topological groups
G
, such as Lie groups, but we do not pursue it here.
. See
Proposition
15
. It is thus a homotopy
d+2
-type.

Since these are fibrations, there is a homotopy-theoretic obstruction to the “lifting problem” for maps into these spaces, such as the
G
-representation map
\alpha
. This motivates the following definition (an analogous definition exists for the unstable anomalies using
{\mathcal{Q}}({\mathcal{A}})
).

Definition 13
.

Beginning with
\alpha^{0}=\alpha
and
k=1
, the
1st anomaly index
is the obstruction to lifting
\alpha^{0}
to

\begin{gathered}\alpha^{1}:BG\to{\mathcal{Q}}^{d,1}_{\mathcal{H}},\end{gathered}

(54)

meaning that

\begin{gathered}p_{1}\circ\alpha^{1}=\alpha^{0}\end{gathered}

(55)

up to homotopy. The obstruction to the existence of this lift is a cohomology class

\begin{gathered}\in H^{1}(BG,\pi_{1}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{\mathcal{H}}),\end{gathered}

(56)

where

\begin{gathered}c^{0}:{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{\mathcal{H}}\to B\pi_{1}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{\mathcal{H}}\end{gathered}

(57)

is the classifying map of the universal cover
{\mathcal{Q}}^{d,1}_{\mathcal{H}}
(see (
52
)).

Continuing this way, after having chosen lifts up to
\alpha^{k-1}
, the
k
th anomaly index
is defined as the obstruction to lifting

\begin{gathered}\alpha^{k-1}:BG\to{\mathcal{Q}}^{d,k-1}_{\mathcal{H}}\end{gathered}

(58)

to

\begin{gathered}\alpha^{k}:BG\to{\mathcal{Q}}^{d,k}_{\mathcal{H}},\end{gathered}

(59)

meaning
p^{k}\circ\alpha^{k}
is homotopy equivalent to
\alpha^{k-1}
. The
k
th anomaly index is a cohomology class

\begin{gathered}\in H^{k}(BG,\pi_{k}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{\mathcal{H}}),\end{gathered}

(60)

where
c^{k-1}:{\mathcal{Q}}^{d,k-1}_{\mathcal{H}}\to B^{k}\pi_{k}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{\mathcal{H}}
is the classifying map of the Whitehead fibration (
52
).

Note that because of the
\Omega
-spectrum property (
Theorem
5
),

\begin{gathered}\pi_{k}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{\mathcal{H}}=\pi_{1}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d-k+1}_{\mathcal{H}},\end{gathered}

(61)

which is a group of stable blend equivalence classes of
d-k+1
-dimensional QCA.

The construction of the first lift corresponds to choosing for each
g
, a blend
\beta(g)
from
\alpha(g)
to the identity. The 1st anomaly index is simply the blend equivalence class of each
\alpha(g)
which obstructs this. The next lift corresponds to choosing a blend from
\beta(g)\beta(h)
to
\beta(gh)
, and so on. At the last stage we need to choose a local operator, and end up getting an obstruction cocycle in
H^{d+2}(BG,U(1))
, which is the index discussed by Else and Nayak
16
. We give a generalization of their construction in
Section
4.2
, showing how to compute the anomaly indices on the lattice.

The use of
\infty
-groupoids is actually necessary for implementing the Else-Nayak proposal, especially beyond one dimension, since the non-abelian structure of QCAs prevents one from considering, eg. a group 2-cocyle valued in QCAs. One needs a definition of
non-abelian cohomology
, which
\infty
-groupoids naturally provide. To appreciate some of the complexity of the higher dimensional anomaly indices, see the recent works
38
;
40
, which gave formulas for the Else-Nayak index in
d=2
(corresponding to what we call the fourth anomaly index).

A subtle point is that the anomaly indices in general
depend on the choice of lifts
. We explore this in detail in
Appendix
9
. Thus, they may not be well-defined given just a QCA
G
-representation. The correct statement from obstruction theory
32
;
49
is as follows.

Theorem 6
.

The (stable) blend anomaly is trivial if and only if there exists a sequence of lifts
\alpha^{k}
such that each anomaly index vanishes.

Proof.

If
\alpha:BG\to{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}
is null-homotopic, then the lifting problem is trivial since we can take all
\alpha^{k}
for
k>0
to be constant maps. Thus, all anomaly indices vanish.

Conversely, if all anomaly indices vanish for a certain sequence of lifts, we have shown that
\alpha
is homotopy equivalent to a constant map
BG\to\star
composed with the sequence of
p^{k}
’s, which altogether is null-homotopic.
∎

Note that the 1st and 2nd anomaly indices are always well-defined (see
Proposition
18
and Appendix
9
).

3
Spectra of Invertible States and SRE Anomalies

3.1
Spectra of Invertible States

In this section we make contact with invertible states. We construct a
\Omega
-spectrum for a subclass of invertible states we call FDQC-invertible, which is closely related to our QCA spectrum, addressing the well-known conjecture
42
(see also
43
;
20
;
4
;
45
).

States may be defined in terms of expectations of local operators, and so we represent them as maps
18
18

18

A linear map on the local algebra
{\mathcal{A}}
extends to the quasi-local algebra iff it is bounded. This will automatically be true for all FDQC-invertible states.

\psi:{\mathcal{A}}\to\mathbb{C}
. This way, we get an action of QCA
\alpha
on states
\psi
by composing
\psi\mapsto\psi\circ\alpha
.

Definition 14
.

A state
\psi:{\mathcal{A}}\to\mathbb{C}
is a
product state
if for all operators
a,b\in{\mathcal{A}}
with disjoint support,
\psi(ab)=\psi(a)\psi(b)
. We say
\psi
is
FDQC-invertible
if it has an inverse state
\tilde{\psi}:{\mathcal{A}}\to\mathbb{C}
such that

\begin{gathered}\psi\otimes\tilde{\psi}=\psi_{0}\circ\alpha_{C}\end{gathered}

(62)

for a product state
\psi_{0}:{\mathcal{A}}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}\to\mathbb{C}
and an FDQC
C
with associated QCA
\alpha_{C}
.
19
19

19

We could also consider the inverse state to live on a different algebra
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}
, but then we could enlarge both algebras to
{\mathcal{A}}\otimes\tilde{\mathcal{A}}
by extending by product states.

In the literature, when discussing invertible states people typically have in mind states
\psi
such that
\psi\otimes\tilde{\psi}
is connected to a product state by some time 1 evolution by a local Hamiltonian with bounded terms. FDQC-invertible states are also invertible in this sense, since we may obtain FDQC by time-dependent Hamiltonian evolution giving each circuit element. However, the classification of this kind of invertible state may be different from that of FDQC-invertible states. It seems likely that one can generalize our methods to include arbitrary Hamiltonian evolution, beginning with a suitable definition of approximate QCA and a notion of blend. Then we would apply the same construction we outline in this section.

The close relationship between QCA and FDQC-invertible states comes from the following observation.

Proposition 7
.

If
\alpha
is a QCA on
{\mathcal{A}}
, then
\alpha\otimes\alpha^{-1}
is a QCA on
{\mathcal{A}}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}
and can be represented as an FDQC

\begin{gathered}\alpha\otimes\alpha^{-1}=(\prod_{n}S_{n})(\alpha^{-1}\otimes 1)(\prod_{x}S_{x})(\alpha\otimes 1)=\prod_{x}S_{x}\prod_{x}(\alpha\otimes 1)(S_{x})\end{gathered}

(63)

where
S_{x}
is the local swap gate at site
x
, which swaps the site algebras
{\mathcal{A}}_{x}
in each of the two tensor factors.

Proof.

This argument is well-known
2
. The
S_{x}
are all commuting, so the same is true for
(\alpha\otimes 1)(S_{x})
. Since
\alpha\otimes 1
has bounded spread, we can thus stagger these gates in a finite depth manner to get a circuit.
∎

Corollary 2
.

If
\psi_{0}:{\mathcal{A}}\to\mathbb{C}
is a product state, then
\psi_{0}\circ\alpha
is an FDQC invertible state, with inverse
\psi_{0}\circ\alpha^{-1}
.

Definition 15
.

A state
\psi:{\mathcal{A}}\to\mathbb{C}
is
QCA-entanglable
if it is of the form
\psi_{0}\circ\alpha
for some product state
\psi_{0}
.

It is not clear if all FDQC-invertible states are QCA-entanglable. For instance,
\psi_{0}
admits a commuting projector Hamiltonian
H_{0}
which is made of single-site projectors onto
\psi_{0}
. This Hamiltonian is gapped and has
\psi_{0}
as its unique ground state, and so we call it a parent Hamiltonian. Applying a QCA
\alpha
, we obtain
\alpha^{-1}(H_{0})
, which is a commuting projector parent Hamiltonian for
\psi_{0}\circ\alpha
. It is known in the broader context of invertible states that some invertible states, namely those with non-zero thermal Hall conductance, such as a Chern insulator, do not admit almost-local commuting projector parent Hamiltonians
67
. They are thus not almost-local-unitary-entanglable. We will see when we include symmetries that there are likely examples of states which are symmetrically FDQC-invertible but not symmetrically QCA-entanglable.
20
20

20

In particular, QCA-entanglable SPTs come with a bulk boundary correspondence, see
Section
3.3
, and there is a mismatch between the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{8}
group of 2+1d
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
SPTs
61
;
39
and 1d
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
blend anomalies, by our calculations in
Section
5
. If these 2d SPTs are really FDQC-invertible (which has not been shown, but seems likely), then they are thus not QCA-entanglable.

On the other hand, we can exploit the following well-known trick:

Proposition 8
.

(the swindle)
Let
\psi:{\mathcal{A}}\to\mathbb{C}
be an FDQC-invertible state and let
\psi_{0}:{\mathcal{A}}\to\mathbb{C}
be any product state. There is an FDQC
\alpha
on

\begin{gathered}\tilde{\mathcal{A}}=\bigotimes_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}}{\mathcal{A}}\end{gathered}

(64)

such that

\begin{gathered}(\psi_{0}\otimes\psi_{0}\otimes\cdots)\circ\alpha=(\psi\otimes\psi_{0}\otimes\psi_{0}\otimes\cdots).\end{gathered}

(65)

Proof.

This mimics another well-known argument called the Eilenberg-Mazur swindle, adapted to the setting of invertible states by Kitaev
42
. Since
\psi
is FDQC-invertible, for each
n
, we can find an FDQC
C_{n}
acting on the
n
th and
(n+1)
st tensor factors of
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}
, and takes
\psi_{0}\otimes\psi_{0}
to
\psi\otimes\tilde{\psi}
, where
\tilde{\psi}
is some inverse of
\psi
. We can also find an FDQC
D_{n}
acting on the
n
th and
(n+1)
st tensor factors taking
\tilde{\psi}\otimes\psi
to
\psi_{0}\otimes\psi_{0}
. We let
\alpha
be defined by the FDQC (see
Figure
7
)

\begin{gathered}\prod_{n\geq 0}D_{2n+1}\prod_{n\geq 0}C_{2n}.\end{gathered}

(66)

By construction, it satisfies (
65
).
∎

Figure 7:
The swindle circuit which acting on a product state
\psi_{0}\otimes\psi_{0}\otimes\psi_{0}\otimes\cdots
produces
\psi\otimes\psi_{0}\otimes\psi_{0}\otimes\cdots
where
\psi
is an arbitrary FDQC-invertible state. This circuit allows us to study FDQC-invertible states which are not necessarily QCA entanglable. If we extend this circuit infinitely in both directions, we get a circuit which fixes the product state
\psi_{0}
, but which does not admit a truncation fixing
\psi_{0}
(unless
\psi
is blend equivalent to
\psi_{0}
, see
Theorem
9
). This is the basis for our cofiber construction of the
\Omega
-spectrum of FDQC-invertible states.

This motivates the following definition.

Definition 16
.

Let
{\mathcal{H}}
be a fixed site Hilbert space, and consider stable
d
-QCA acting on the lattice
{\mathbb{Z}}^{\omega}
built from
{\mathcal{H}}
. Let
\psi_{0}
be a fixed state in
{\mathcal{H}}
, which defines a product state
\bar{\psi}_{0}
on
{\mathbb{Z}}^{\omega}
. We can define the
\Omega
-spectrum

\begin{gathered}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{{\mathcal{H}},\psi_{0}}\end{gathered}

(67)

of stable
d
-QCA, blends, and so on, all of which are required to fix
21
21

21

Local unitaries are required to fix the state exactly, not just up to phases.
the chosen product state
\bar{\psi}_{0}
. This has a natural map of spectra

\begin{gathered}\Omega_{\star}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{{\mathcal{H}},\psi_{0}}\to\Omega_{\star}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{\mathcal{H}},\end{gathered}

(68)

and we let

\begin{gathered}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{{\mathcal{H}},inv}=\lim_{k\to\infty}\Omega^{k}(\Omega_{\star}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d+k}_{\mathcal{H}}/\Omega_{\star}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d+k}_{{\mathcal{H}},\psi_{0}})\end{gathered}

(69)

be the cofiber
\Omega
-spectrum. We will show in
Theorem
9
how
{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{inv}
can be regarded an
\Omega
-spectrum of FDQC-invertible states.

Under this definition, the swindle FDQC constructed in
Proposition
8
can be regarded as a blend from the identity to something equivalent to the identity, since its “tail” in (
66
) fixes
\psi_{0}
for
n>1
(see also
Figure
7
). Thus, it corresponds to a loop in
\Omega_{\star}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d+1}
relative to
\Omega_{\star}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d+1}_{\psi_{0}}
. By the definition of the cofiber spectrum, this yields an element of
{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{inv}
.
22
22

22

Since the swindle constructs an FDQC, one may be worried that the construction can blend to the identity, and so we have made something trivial. However, we are not guaranteed to be able to blend the “tail” of (
66
) along any axis in a way that still fixes
\psi_{0}
. Therefore, these naive blends do not produce lower dimensional blends in the cofiber spectrum, even though we did get a lower dimensional QCA. From a homotopy point of view, this blend may yield a non-trivial element of the relative homotopy group
\pi_{1}(\Omega_{\star}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d+1}_{\mathcal{H}},\Omega_{\star}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d+1}_{{\mathcal{H}},\psi_{0}},1)
.
. This construction also does not depend on the product state
\psi_{0}
, because any two choices are related by an on-site change of basis, which gives an automorphism of
{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}
respecting blending, and hence the
\infty
-groupoid and spectrum structure.

To recap, we obtain FDQC-invertible states by applying QCA to
\bar{\psi}_{0}
. By taking the cofiber spectrum
{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{inv}
we quotient out (in the correct spectrum sense) by precisely those QCA which send
\bar{\psi}_{0}
to itself. Because of the swindle, this simultaneously gives us access to all FDQC-invertible states. This seems to be good evidence that
{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{inv}
can be interpreted as a
\Omega
-spectrum of FDQC-invertible states, which we now pursue.

To prove a correspondence with FDQC-invertible states, we need a definition of state which is suitable for the stable setting where the QCA spectrum exists.

Definition 17
.

We consider the algebra
{\mathcal{A}}^{\omega}_{\mathcal{H}}
on
{\mathbb{Z}}^{\omega}
with site Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}}
. A
stable
d
-state
is a state
\psi:{\mathcal{A}}^{\omega}_{\mathcal{H}}\to\mathbb{C}
such that there exists a triple of non-negative integers
a,b,l
defining the
domain of
\psi

\begin{gathered}D(\psi)={\mathbb{Z}}^{d}\times[a,b]^{l}\times 0\times\cdots\end{gathered}

(70)

such that

\begin{gathered}\psi=\psi_{D}\otimes\bar{\psi}_{0},\end{gathered}

(71)

where
\psi_{D}
is a state on
{\mathcal{A}}^{\omega}_{\mathcal{H}}(D(\psi))
and
\bar{\psi}_{0}
is the product state
\psi_{0}
on all other sites.
\psi
is thus determined by the state
\psi_{D}
on its domain, which is a finitely-thickened
d
-dimensional lattice. A stable
d
-state is
FDQC-invertible
if
\psi_{D}
is FDQC-invertible.

A
blend of stable
d
-states

\psi\equiv_{i}\psi^{\prime}
is a stable
d
-state
\psi^{\prime\prime}
whose domain contains both the domains of
\psi
and
\psi^{\prime}
, and which, considered as a map
\psi^{\prime\prime}:{\mathcal{A}}^{\omega}_{\mathcal{H}}(D(\psi^{\prime\prime}))\to\mathbb{C}
, equals
\psi\otimes\bar{\psi}_{0}
to the left of the blend interface, and
\psi^{\prime}\otimes\bar{\psi}_{0}
to the right of the blend interface. Here
\bar{\psi}_{0}
denotes padding with the product state
\psi_{0}
on sites of
D(\psi^{\prime\prime})-D(\psi)
and
D(\psi^{\prime\prime})-D(\psi^{\prime})
in each respective case.

Theorem 9
.

The path components of
{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{{\mathcal{H}},inv}
can be identified as

\begin{gathered}\pi_{0}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{{\mathcal{H}},inv}=\left\{\text{FDQC-invertible stable $d$-states}\right\}/\sim,\end{gathered}

(72)

where
\sim
is blend equivalence along the
d
th axis. These equivalence classes form an abelian group under stacking. Furthermore, there is a long exact sequence for each
n
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n+1}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d-1}_{{\mathcal{H}},\psi_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n+1}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{{\mathcal{H}},\psi_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n+1}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{{\mathcal{H}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{{\mathcal{H}},inv}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n+1}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d+1}_{{\mathcal{H}},\psi_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}

(73)

where for
n=0
,
i
is the inclusion of QCA fixing
\psi_{0}
into all QCA;
\psi_{0}:\alpha\mapsto\bar{\psi}_{0}\circ\alpha
is the map from QCA to FDQC-invertible states; and
s
is the swindle construction of
Proposition
8
, which from an FDQC-invertible state produces an FDQC in one higher dimension which fixes
\psi_{0}
and creates
\psi
when it is appropriately truncated.

This proof is somewhat involved, and uses some of the details of the construction of the QCA spectrum we develop in
Section
4
. The proof can be found in
Appendix
7
.

3.2
Anomalies as obstructions to trivial symmetric states

Let us return to the question of anomalies. We have so far studied the homotopy class of maps

\begin{gathered}\alpha:BG\to{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{{\mathcal{H}}}\end{gathered}

(74)

as obstructions to stably disentangling QCA
G
-representations. However, we may also be interested in whether such a symmetry admits a symmetric short-range-entangled (SRE) state, meaning a state which is created by an FDQC from a product state. If it does not, then all symmetric states must be long-range-entangled (LRE) (i.e. not SRE). From the point of view of predicting the ground state of a symmetric Hamiltonian, this is typically the more interesting question. A classic lattice theorem along these lines is the Lieb-Schultz-Mattis theorem
46
. This has been interpreted as an anomaly by many authors, see eg.
11
;
15
;
10
. From the point of view of quantum field theory, we expect that ’t Hooft anomalies play a dual role as obstructions to gauging as well as obstructions to trivial symmetric states. As emphasized by
64
;
57
, these concepts are different on the lattice, since there are symmetries with non-trivial blend anomalies which admit symmetric SRE states (see
Example
1
below).

We would thus like to use homotopy theory to formulate another kind of lattice anomaly, which we will call the “SRE anomaly”, which is an obstruction to having a symmetric SRE state. This motivates the following definition.

Definition 18
.

Let
{\mathcal{H}}
be a fixed site Hilbert space, and
\alpha:BG\to{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{\mathcal{H}}
a stable
d
-QCA
G
-representation. We define its
SRE anomaly
as the (based) homotopy class of the induced map

\begin{gathered}BG\xrightarrow{\alpha}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{\mathcal{H}}\to B{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{{\mathcal{H}},inv},\end{gathered}

(75)

given by delooping the cofiber map

\begin{gathered}\Omega_{\star}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{{\mathcal{H}}}\to{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{{\mathcal{H}},inv}.\end{gathered}

(76)

This definition satisfies many of the nice properties that the stable blend anomaly satisfied, but now applicable to the problem of finding symmetric SRE states.

Theorem 10
.

With the notation as in
Definition
18
, the SRE anomalies of stable
d
-QCA
G
-representations form an abelian group

\begin{gathered}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d+1}_{{\mathcal{H}},inv}(BG,\star)\end{gathered}

(77)

(a reduced generalized cohomology theory) such that

1.

The group structure corresponds to stacking

\begin{gathered}=[\alpha]+[\beta],\end{gathered}

(78)

2.

Any stably-disentanglable
G
-representation
\alpha
has
[\alpha]=0

3.

The reversed
G
-representation
\alpha^{\text{rev}}
obtained by reflection along the
d
th axis (as in
Corollary
1
) has

\begin{gathered}=-[\alpha].\end{gathered}

(79)

4.

Furthermore, if there is a stable
d
-state of the form

\begin{gathered}\psi=\bar{\psi}_{0}\circ\alpha_{C}\end{gathered}

(80)

for some FDQC
C
(such a state may be called
FDQC-SRE
) which is moreover fixed by the action of
G
, so that for each
g
,

\begin{gathered}\psi\circ\alpha(g)=\psi,\end{gathered}

(81)

then the SRE anomaly vanishes, i.e.

\begin{gathered}=0\in{\mathcal{Q}}^{d+1}_{{\mathcal{H}},inv}(BG,\star).\end{gathered}

(82)

In other words, if the SRE anomaly is non-vanishing,
\alpha
does not admit symmetric FDQC-SRE states.

5.

Finally, we have a long exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{{\mathcal{H}},inv}(BG,\star)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{{\mathcal{H}},\psi_{0}}(BG)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(BG)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Q}}^{d+1}_{{\mathcal{H}},inv}(BG,\star)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Q}}^{d+1}_{{\mathcal{H}},\psi_{0}}(BG)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces SRE}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces SRE}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}

(83)

where
SRE(\alpha)
gives the SRE anomaly of a QCA
G
-representation. Its kernel is the image under
i
of those QCA
G
-representations fixing a product state
\bar{\psi}_{0}
and its cokernel are SRE anomalies not realizable by QCA
G
-representations, see
Section
5
. The connecting homomorphism
s
is given by the swindle circuit of
Proposition
8
, which fixes the product state and has
G
-symmetric gates so it is in the kernel of
i
.

Proof.

The abelian group structure comes because
{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{{\mathcal{H}},inv}
is an
\Omega
-spectrum, so
B{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{{\mathcal{H}},inv}
is an infinite loop space. We have used this structure to identify the SRE anomalies, which are the homotopy classes of maps
BG\to B{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{{\mathcal{H}},inv}
, with the reduced cohomology in one higher dimension (written as cohomology relative to the point
\star
):

\begin{gathered}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d+1}_{{\mathcal{H}},inv}(BG,\star).\end{gathered}

(84)

This is an indication of a bulk-boundary correspondence we return to in
Section
3.3
. The three properties of the group structure follow because
\alpha:BG\to B{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{{\mathcal{H}},inv}
factors through
\alpha:BG\to{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{\mathcal{H}}
. See the analogous properties for the non-symmetric case discussed in
Section
2.3
.

Now suppose there is a symmetric FDQC-SRE state
\psi=\bar{\psi}_{0}\circ\alpha_{C}
. The
G
-representation
\alpha_{C}\alpha(g)\alpha_{C}^{-1}
thus fixes the product state
\bar{\psi}_{0}
. Since
C
is a FDQC,
\alpha_{C}\alpha(g)\alpha_{C}^{-1}
as a blend of
G
-representations to
\alpha(g)
, so they are homotopic by the argument in
Lemma 1
. This means we can homotope
\alpha:BG\to{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{{\mathcal{H}}}
into
{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{{\mathcal{H}},\psi_{0}}
, so
\alpha
is null-homotopic in the cofiber.

Finally, the long exact sequence is the cofiber long exact sequence for

\begin{gathered}B\Omega_{\star}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d+1}_{{\mathcal{H}},\psi_{0}}\to B\Omega_{\star}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d+1}_{{\mathcal{H}}}\to B{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{{\mathcal{H}},inv}\end{gathered}

(85)

combined with
B\Omega_{\star}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d+1}_{\mathcal{H}}={\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{\mathcal{H}}
, and likewise for
{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{{\mathcal{H}},\psi_{0}}
. Recall this is equivalent to the spectrum property
\Omega_{id}\Omega_{\star}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d+1}_{\mathcal{H}}=\Omega_{\star}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{\mathcal{H}}
.
∎

Example 1
.

Consistent with this theory, it has recently been observed that there are non-stably-disentanglable symmetries which nonetheless admit symmetric product states
57
;
64
. These should be regarded as being in the kernel of the map
SRE
. One example, constructed in
57
, is an FDQC
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
-representation
\alpha(0)=1
,
\alpha(1)=\alpha_{C}
, where
C
is a particular FDQC satisfying
\alpha_{C}^{2}=1
but having a truncation
C^{\prime}
to a half-space that has
\alpha_{C^{\prime}}^{2}
equal to a unit translation along the boundary of the half-space composed with an FDQC.

We can regard the truncated circuit as a blend
\beta(1)=\alpha_{C^{\prime}}:\alpha(1)\equiv_{2}1
. The 1st anomaly index of
Section
2.4
vanishes because we are able to choose this blend (
\alpha(0)
is the identity so we can use itself as a blend,
\beta(0)=\alpha(0)
). The 2nd anomaly index measures whether these blends satisfy the group law up to 2-blends (see
Section
4.2
). The two-cocycle of interest is

\begin{gathered}\beta_{2}(1,1)=\beta(1)\beta(1)\beta(0)^{-1}=\beta(1)^{2},\end{gathered}

(86)

which by its construction involves a translation along the first axis. Thus, it does not admit a blend to the identity along this axis.

We can of course choose different truncations, so the obstruction is the cohomology class of

\begin{gathered}\in H^{2}(B{\mathbb{Z}}_{2},{\mathbb{Z}})={\mathbb{Z}}_{2}.\end{gathered}

(87)

In particular, we can choose different
\beta(1)
and see if one of them does result in a blendable
\beta(1)^{2}
. We should not have to change
\beta(0)
because we can work with normalized group 2-cocycles
5
. We can for instance add a translation in the blend region to
\beta(1)
, but this changes
\beta(1)^{2}
by a double translation, which is why we have a
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
invariant above. In this case, since
\beta(1)^{2}
is a unit translation,
[\beta_{2}]\neq 0
. This anomaly-index does not depend on choices (see
Proposition
18
), so it follows that

\begin{gathered}\neq 0\in{\mathcal{Q}}^{2}_{\mathcal{H}}(BG),\end{gathered}

(88)

so
\alpha
is not stably-disentanglable.

On the other hand,
\alpha
does admit symmetric product states
57
;
64
so it should have vanishing SRE anomaly. We can at least show that the 2nd anomaly index computed above does not yield an SRE anomaly. We could in fact define a whole sequence of
SRE anomaly indices
by studying the Whitehead tower of
B{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{{\mathcal{H}},inv}
, exactly as in
Section
2.4
and
Section
4.2
. We would find
[\beta_{2}]
as above, but we are only interested in its image under the map induced by the cofiber sequence (
73
):

\begin{gathered}H^{2}(B{\mathbb{Z}}_{2},\pi_{2}{\mathcal{Q}}^{2}_{\mathcal{H}})\to H^{2}(B{\mathbb{Z}}_{2},\pi_{1}{\mathcal{Q}}^{2}_{{\mathcal{H}},inv}).\end{gathered}

(89)

\pi_{2}{\mathcal{Q}}^{2}_{\mathcal{H}}=\pi_{1}{\mathcal{Q}}^{1}_{\mathcal{H}}
is generated by a translation along the first axis, which fixes
\bar{\psi}_{0}
, so it is in the image of

\begin{gathered}\pi_{2}{\mathcal{Q}}^{2}_{{\mathcal{H}},\psi_{0}}\to\pi_{2}{\mathcal{Q}}^{2}_{\mathcal{H}}\end{gathered}

(90)

and therefore the map

\begin{gathered}\pi_{2}{\mathcal{Q}}^{2}_{\mathcal{H}}\to\pi_{1}{\mathcal{Q}}^{2}_{{\mathcal{H}},inv}\end{gathered}

(91)

is zero. In fact, with a little more work using the Atiyah-Hirzebruch spectral sequence, one can show
{\mathcal{Q}}^{2}_{{\mathcal{H}},inv}(B{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})=0
so the SRE anomaly is trivial.

3.3
Classifying SPTs and Bulk-Boundary Correspondence

In this section, we add global on-site symmetries to the discussion of
Section
3.1
, giving a spectrum of invertible
G
-symmetric states, and discussing bulk-boundary correspondences connecting SPTs with blend and SRE anomalies. However, we will see the bulk-boundary correspondences on the lattice are likely not one-to-one.

Let
{\mathcal{H}}
be a site Hilbert space carrying a representation of a group
G
. We consider QCAs on the local algebra built from
{\mathcal{H}}
, which inherits an on-site
G
action. We would like to classify QCAs
\alpha
commuting with
G
, up to blends commuting with
G
, as well as
G
-symmetric FDQC-invertible states, up to blends of such states. These will have associated
\Omega
-spectra that are related in much the same way as we saw in
Section
3.1
. In fact these spectra will be embedded inside the previous ones as the fixed points of a
G
action.

Indeed, when we construct the space
{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{\mathcal{H}}
, it inherits an action of
G
, since given a stable
d
-QCA
\alpha
, we obtain another stable
d
-QCA
g\circ\alpha\circ g^{-1}
by taking
g\in G
to act diagonally on all the copies
{\mathcal{H}}
in the domain of
\alpha
. More precisely, suppose we have a stable
d
-QCA
\alpha
with a domain
D(\alpha)
which describes an algebra with site Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}}
on a thickened
d
-dimensional lattice. We obtain a
G
action on
D(\alpha)
by tensor product

\begin{gathered}g_{D(\alpha)}=\bigotimes_{x\in D(\alpha)}g_{x},\end{gathered}

(92)

where
g_{x}
is the action on the copy of
{\mathcal{H}}
at site
x
. Then

\begin{gathered}g_{D(\alpha)}\circ\alpha\circ g_{D(\alpha)}^{-1}\end{gathered}

(93)

is another stable
d
-QCA with the same domain.

This action is compatible with blends and their composition, including those implemented by local unitaries. Thus, we get a cellular action of
G
on
{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{\mathcal{H}}
which has the property that if
g
fixes a cell, it fixes it pointwise. This makes it a
G
-CW complex
25
. We denote the fixed points of this
G
-action as

\begin{gathered}({\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{\mathcal{H}})^{G}=\{\sigma\in{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{\mathcal{H}}\ |\ \forall g,\ g(\sigma)=g\},\end{gathered}

(94)

where
\sigma
denotes any cell of
{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{\mathcal{H}}
. Because of the nice
G
action, this is a sub-simplicial complex of
{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{\mathcal{H}}
. It is exactly what we would build from our construction of
{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{\mathcal{H}}
if we instead only include QCAs and blends which commute with the on-site
G
action. We encode this in the following:

Definition 19
.

Let
{\mathcal{H}}
be a site Hilbert space carrying a representation of a group
G
.

1.

A
stable
G
-
d
-QCA
is a stable
d
-QCA
\alpha
on a domain
D(\alpha)\subset{\mathbb{Z}}^{\omega}
, commuting with the induced tensor product
G
action on
{\mathcal{A}}^{\omega}_{\mathcal{H}}(D(\alpha))
.

2.

A
G
-blend of stable
G
-
d
-QCA
is a blend which is itself a stable
G
-
d
-QCA.

3.

We obtain the
space of stable
G
-
d
-QCA

({\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{\mathcal{H}})^{G}
as the fixed points of the induced
G
action on
{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{\mathcal{H}}
.

Proposition 11
.

Let
{\mathcal{H}}
be a site Hilbert space carrying a representation of a group
G
.

1.

The
G
-action on
{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{\mathcal{H}}
commutes with the
\Omega
-spectrum maps (which are inclusions) and so
\Omega_{\star}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{\mathcal{H}}
defines a (naive)
G
-
\Omega
-spectrum in the sense of
25
.

2.

The
G
-fixed points
\Omega_{\star}({\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{\mathcal{H}})^{G}
define a sub-
\Omega
-spectrum of
{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{\mathcal{H}}
.

3.

\pi_{1}(({\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{\mathcal{H}})^{G})
is the group of stable
G
-
d
-QCAs up to
G
-blend equivalence along the
d
th axis, with the group operation given by stacking.

4.

The inclusion of the fixed points induces a “forgetful map”

\begin{gathered}F_{G}:\pi_{1}(({\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{\mathcal{H}})^{G})\to\pi_{1}({\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{\mathcal{H}})\end{gathered}

(95)

which takes a
G
-blend equivalence class of
G
-QCA to the blend equivalence class of the QCA, forgetting the
G
structure.

As mentioned above, we can consider stable
G
-
d
-QCA as entanglers for certain
G
-symmetric stable
d
-states when applied to product states, analogous to how we considered QCA-entanglable states above. These states will be invertible in a
G
-symmetric sense as follows:

Definition 20
.

Let
{\mathcal{H}}
be a site Hilbert space carrying a representation of a group
G
with a vector
\psi_{0}
with
g\cdot\psi_{0}=\psi_{0}
. We consider stable
d
-states on the algebra
{\mathcal{A}}^{\omega}_{\mathcal{H}}
, which inherits an action of
G
. A
G
-symmetric stable
d
-state
\psi
is
G
-FDQC invertible
if there is another
G
-symmetric stable
d
-state
\tilde{\psi}
such that

\begin{gathered}\psi\otimes\tilde{\psi}=\bar{\psi}_{0}\circ\alpha_{C},\end{gathered}

(96)

where
\alpha_{C}
is a QCA associated to a FDQC
C
with
G
-symmetric gates, which we call a
G
-FDQC
and
\bar{\psi}_{0}
is a product state made from
\psi_{0}
on a suitable domain.

It is important to require the individual gates of the FDQC being each
G
-symmetric, rather then just the FDQC commuting with
G
as a whole. One reason is that FDQC with
G
-symmetric gates admit
G
-blends by truncating the circuit. It may be that an FDQC which commutes with
G
as a QCA defines a non-trivial
G
-QCA. These will represent non-trivial elements of the kernel of the forgetful map
F_{G}
in (
95
). These are the entanglers of “true SPTs” which are trivial invertible states only once the symmetry is forgotten, and we want to consider true SPTs as non-trivial.

With this definition we have the analog of
Theorem
9
, giving us a spectrum of
G
-FDQC invertible stable
d
-states:

Theorem 12
.

Let
{\mathcal{H}}
be a site Hilbert space carrying a representation of a group
G
with a vector
\psi_{0}
with
g\cdot\psi_{0}=\psi_{0}
.

1.

If
\alpha
is a
G
-QCA, then
\alpha\otimes\alpha^{-1}
can be written as a
G
-FDQC.

2.

If
\psi
is a
G
-FDQC invertible stable
d
-state, then there is a
G
-FDQC on a
d+1
-dimensional half-space which produces
\psi
at its boundary when applied to
\bar{\psi}_{0}
.

3.

We obtain a
G
-action on the space
{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{{\mathcal{H}},\psi_{0}}
of stable
d
-QCAs
\alpha
fixing the product state
\bar{\psi}_{0}=\bigotimes_{x\in D(\alpha)}\psi_{0}
on their domain, and this defines a naive
G
-
\Omega
-spectrum, with the inclusion
\Omega_{\star}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{{\mathcal{H}},\psi_{0}}\to\Omega_{\star}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{{\mathcal{H}}}
an equivariant map of naive
G
-
\Omega
-spectra.

4.

Let
{\mathcal{Q}}^{d,G}_{{\mathcal{H}},inv}
be the cofiber spectrum of the inclusion

\begin{gathered}\Omega_{\star}({\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{{\mathcal{H}},\psi_{0}})^{G}\to\Omega_{\star}({\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{G}\to{\mathcal{Q}}^{d,G}_{{\mathcal{H}},inv}.\end{gathered}

(97)

This is equivalent to the
G
-fixed points of the induced
G
-action on
{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{{\mathcal{H}},inv}
:

\begin{gathered}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d,G}_{{\mathcal{H}},inv}=({\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{{\mathcal{H}},inv})^{G}.\end{gathered}

(98)

We henceforth use the notation on the LHS for fixed points of these spectra since it is unambiguous.

5.

\pi_{0}({\mathcal{Q}}^{d,G}_{{\mathcal{H}},G})
is the abelian group of
G
-FDQC invertible stable
d
-states up to blend equivalence along the first axis, with the group structure given by stacking.

6.

There is a long exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n+1}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d-1,G}_{{\mathcal{H}},\psi_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n+1}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d,G}_{{\mathcal{H}},\psi_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n+1}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d,G}_{{\mathcal{H}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d,G}_{{\mathcal{H}},inv}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n+1}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d+1,G}_{{\mathcal{H}},\psi_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}

(99)

as in (
73
).

7.

The inclusion of fixed points induces a forgetful map

\begin{gathered}F_{G}:\pi_{0}({\mathcal{Q}}^{d,G}_{{\mathcal{H}},inv})\to\pi_{0}({\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{{\mathcal{H}},inv}).\end{gathered}

(100)

Elements of the kernel of this map are equivalence classes of true SPTs up to blend equivalence along the
d
th axis (compare (
95
)).

Proof.

The argument for the first statement is the same as in
Proposition
7
, once noting that the local swap gates are
G
-symmetric, and
\alpha\otimes 1
sends
G
-symmetric gates to
G
-symmetric gates since it commutes with the
G
action. The second statement follows from the argument of
Proposition
8
exactly, noting now that the
C
and
D
circuits are
G
-FDQC. The third and fourth points follow from the nice cellular action of
G
, which makes the inclusion a
G
-cofibration
25
. The proof of the fifth point is exactly as in the proof of
Theorem
9
, described in
Appendix
7
, except now we use the swindle
G
-circuit. For the sixth point, this is the cofiber long exact sequence. For the last point, the map is induced by the commutative square of inclusions:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Q}}_{{\mathcal{H}},\psi_{0}}^{d,G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Q}}_{{\mathcal{H}}}^{d,G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Q}}_{{\mathcal{H}},\psi_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Q}}_{{\mathcal{H}}}^{d}}

(101)

∎

It is generally expected that true SPTs (meaning those which realize trivial invertible phases when
G
is forgotten) in
d
dimensions should correspond to anomalies in
d-1
dimensions. However, it is not so obvious how to define this bulk-boundary correspondence on the lattice. Furthermore, our calculation of
{\mathcal{Q}}^{1}_{f,inv}(B{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})={\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{4}
in
Section
5
does not match the expected
{\mathbb{Z}}_{8}
classification of 2+1d fermionic
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
true SPTs (see
61
). It thus seems likely that there is no general bulk-boundary correspondence relating true SPTs to SRE anomalies.

However, there is a way to relate SPT entanglers to blend anomalies. Suppose our SPT
\psi
admits a QCA entangler, meaning there is a
G
-QCA
\alpha
such that
\psi=\psi_{0}\circ\alpha
. Suppose also that
\alpha
admits a blend to the identity, so that
\psi
is blend-trivial as an invertible state. It is thus a pure SPT. For these states we can express a bulk-boundary correspondence, along the lines of
7
;
68
;
57
. We phrase it in terms of an anomaly-free QCA
G
-representation
\sigma(g)
and a blendable QCA
\alpha
which commutes with it, yielding a map

\begin{gathered}(\sigma,\alpha):BG\times B{\mathbb{Z}}\to{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{\mathcal{H}}.\end{gathered}

(102)

We can study the homotopy class
[\sigma,\alpha]
of this map. We find there is a “bulk-boundary correspondence” putting these homotopy classes in one to one correspondence with stable blend anomalies of QCA
G
-representations in
d-1
-dimensions:

Theorem 13
.

The homotopy classes of maps

\begin{gathered}:BG\times B{\mathbb{Z}}\to{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{\mathcal{H}}\end{gathered}

(103)

such that
[\sigma]
and
[\alpha]
separately are homotopically trivial, are in one-to-one correspondence with homotopy classes of maps

\begin{gathered}\beta:BG\to{\mathcal{Q}}^{d-1}_{\mathcal{H}}.\end{gathered}

(104)

Proof.

Maps
BG\times B{\mathbb{Z}}\to{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{\mathcal{H}}
are equivalent to maps

\begin{gathered}BG\to\text{Maps}(B{\mathbb{Z}},{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{\mathcal{H}}).\end{gathered}

(105)

Since
B{\mathbb{Z}}=S^{1}
, the latter is the free loop space
L{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{\mathcal{H}}
. It sits in a fibration

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega_{\star}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{\mathcal{H}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{\mathcal{H}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{\mathcal{H}}}

(106)

Since
\Omega_{\star}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{\mathcal{H}}
is an infinite loop space, this fibration splits (see
47
exercise 2.24), giving us

\begin{gathered}L{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{\mathcal{H}}=\Omega_{\star}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{\mathcal{H}}\times{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{\mathcal{H}}.\end{gathered}

(107)

Thus, homotopy classes of maps
BG\times B{\mathbb{Z}}\to{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{\mathcal{H}}
are given by a pair of homotopy classes of a map

\begin{gathered}\sigma:BG\to{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{\mathcal{H}},\end{gathered}

(108)

representing the stable blend anomaly of
\sigma
, which is assumed to be trivial, and a homotopy class

\begin{gathered}\phi:BG\to\Omega_{\star}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{\mathcal{H}}.\end{gathered}

(109)

By construction,
\phi
sends the basepoint of
BG
to

\begin{gathered}\alpha:B{\mathbb{Z}}=S^{1}\to{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{\mathcal{H}}.\end{gathered}

(110)

As this is assumed to be null-homotopic as well,
\phi
lands in the component of the identity
d
-QCA, and that component is homotopy equivalent to
{\mathcal{Q}}^{d-1}_{\mathcal{H}}
. The result follows.
∎

To get some intuition for this theorem, we can represent the free loop space
L{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}
as an
\infty
-groupoid whose objects are diagrams in
{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{\mathcal{H}}

\begin{gathered}\star\xrightarrow{\alpha}\star\end{gathered}

(111)

and whose 1-morphisms are diagrams

\text{Hom}(\star\xrightarrow{\alpha}\star,\star\xrightarrow{\beta}\star)=\hbox to71.22pt{\vbox to48.22pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 35.61107pt\lower-25.54025pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

(112)

with composition by vertical pasting. Note this differs from the based loop space
\Omega_{\star}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{\mathcal{H}}
for which the 1-morphisms must have
\gamma=\delta=1
and the picture reduces to a 2-globe, as in (
4.2
) (so a morphism only exists between
\alpha
and
\beta
if they admit a blend along the first axis). 2-morphisms are cubes with suitable decorations and so on.

The map
BG\to L{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{\mathcal{H}}
sends its base point
\star
to
\star\xrightarrow{\alpha}\star
and its 1-morphisms
\star\xrightarrow{g}\star
to the 1-morphisms

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-7.5182pt}{-22.41528pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

{{{{{}}{
