\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\qquad\in\text{Hom}(\star\xrightarrow{\alpha}\star,\star\xrightarrow{\alpha}\star)

(113)

where the blend is the identity, encoding the commutation
\sigma(g)\alpha=\alpha\sigma(g)
. Higher morphisms may be filled in with identities as well since
\sigma(g)
satisfy the group laws.

The issue is that when we try to homotope this map into
\Omega_{\star}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{\mathcal{H}}
by blending
\gamma:\alpha\equiv_{1}1
, we may not be able to choose
\gamma
to commute with
\sigma(g)
(i.e. to be a blend of
G
-QCA) so that these higher morphisms will need to be filled in with non-trivial data, representing the lower dimensional anomaly.

Figure 8:
Given an on-site symmetry
\sigma(g)
and a blend
\gamma:\alpha\equiv_{1}1
, where
\alpha
commutes with
\sigma(g)
, we can construct a QCA
G
-representation
\beta(g)
in one lower dimension, which serves as an obstruction to finding a blend
\gamma^{\prime}:\alpha\equiv_{1}1
which commutes with
\sigma(g)
. Considering
\alpha
as an SPT entangler, this construction yields the “anomalous boundary symmetry” of the SPT.

One way to construct the obstruction is as follows. Let
\gamma:\alpha\equiv_{1}1
. Consider

\begin{gathered}\sigma^{\prime}(g)=\gamma\sigma(g)\gamma^{-1}:\sigma(g)\equiv_{1}\sigma(g).\end{gathered}

(114)

Since
\alpha
commutes with
\sigma(g)
, these are blends
\sigma(g)\equiv_{1}\sigma(g)
, and they satisfy the group law since
\sigma(g)
does. Let us now suppose that
\sigma(g)
is on-site (or admits blends of
G
-representations), so that we can truncate the action of
\sigma^{\prime}(g)
to a finite region to form

\begin{gathered}\beta(g):1\equiv_{1}\sigma(g)\equiv_{1}\sigma(g)\equiv_{1}1,\end{gathered}

(115)

which also satisfy the group law, by construction. See
Fig.
8
. The homotopy class of
\beta:BG\to{\mathcal{Q}}^{d-1}_{\mathcal{H}}
is equivalent to the homotopy class of
[\sigma,\alpha]
, and represents an obstruction to finding a blend of
G
-QCA from
\alpha
to the identity. We note that this is precisely the usual construction of the boundary anomalous symmetry from a bulk SPT entangler
7
;
68
;
57
.

4
Constructing a Space of QCA

4.1
Globular Picture and Homotopy Groups

The first step to constructing the
\infty
-groupoid
{\mathcal{Q}}({\mathcal{A}})
will be to construct it as a “globular set”, formalizing the diagrams which were outlined in
Section
2.2
.

Definition 21

(Globular Set of QCA)
.

Let
{\mathcal{A}}
be a local algebra on
{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}
. Let
1\leq k\leq d+1
. We define a globular set
{\mathcal{Q}}({\mathcal{A}})=\{{\mathcal{Q}}({\mathcal{A}})_{n}\}_{0\leq n\leq d+2}
for which the set of
n
-globes
{\mathcal{Q}}({\mathcal{A}})_{n}
is defined as follows

1.

There is a unique 0-globe called
\star
.

2.

A 1-globe is a QCA.

3.

A 2-globe is a pair of QCAs
\varphi_{1},\varphi_{1}^{\prime}
and a blend

\begin{gathered}\varphi_{2}:1\equiv_{1}\varphi_{1}^{\prime}\varphi_{1}^{-1}.\end{gathered}

(116)

We write this as

\begin{gathered}(\varphi_{2}:\varphi_{1}\to_{1}\varphi_{1}^{\prime}),\end{gathered}

(117)

where we introduce the notation
\alpha:\beta\to_{1}\gamma
, meaning
\alpha:1\equiv_{1}\gamma\beta^{-1}
.

4.

For
3\leq k\leq d+1
, a
k
-globe consists of an array

\begin{gathered}\begin{pmatrix}\varphi_{k}&:&\varphi_{k-1}&\rightarrow_{k-1}&\varphi_{k-1}^{\prime}\\
&&&\vdots&\\
&:&\varphi_{2}&\rightarrow_{2}&\varphi_{2}^{\prime}\\
&:&\varphi_{1}&\rightarrow_{1}&\varphi_{1}^{\prime}\end{pmatrix},\end{gathered}

(118)

where we introduce the notation
\alpha:\beta\to_{n}\gamma
to mean

\begin{gathered}\alpha:1\equiv_{m}1\quad\forall m<n\\
\alpha:1\equiv_{n}\gamma\beta^{-1}.\end{gathered}

(119)

The array is shorthand to mean for each
1\leq n\leq k
, our
k
-globe has

\begin{gathered}\varphi_{n+1}:\varphi_{n}\to_{n}\varphi_{n},\end{gathered}

(120)

and likewise for each
1\leq n<k
,

\begin{gathered}\varphi_{n+1}^{\prime}:\varphi_{n}\to_{n}\varphi_{n}^{\prime}.\end{gathered}

(121)

Note that
\varphi_{n+1}
and
\varphi_{n+1}^{\prime}
are supported in
[a,b]^{n-1}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq c}\times{\mathbb{Z}}^{d-n}
, where
[a,b]
is some interval and
c\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. In particular, for
k=d+1
,
\varphi_{d+1}
is supported in a finite region
[a,b]^{d}
.

5.

For
k=d+2
a
d+2
-globe is an array

\begin{gathered}\begin{pmatrix}U&:&\varphi_{d+1}&\rightarrow_{d+1}&\varphi_{d+1}^{\prime}\\
&&&\vdots&\\
&:&\varphi_{2}&\rightarrow_{2}&\varphi_{2}^{\prime}\\
&:&\varphi_{1}&\rightarrow_{1}&\varphi_{1}^{\prime}\end{pmatrix},\end{gathered}

(122)

where for a local unitary
U
,

\begin{gathered}U:\varphi_{d+1}\to_{d+1}\varphi_{d+1}^{\prime}\end{gathered}

(123)

has the special meaning

\begin{gathered}\text{Ad}\ U=\varphi_{d+1}^{\prime}\varphi_{d+1}^{-1}\end{gathered}

(124)

(note the RHS is supported in a finite region). To simplify the notation we also let

\begin{gathered}U:\alpha\equiv_{d+1}\beta\end{gathered}

(125)

mean
{\rm Ad}\ U=\beta\alpha^{-1}
.

To be a globular set we must define maps (which can be thought of as “source” and “target”, respectively)

\begin{gathered}\sigma_{k},\tau_{k}:{\mathcal{Q}}({\mathcal{A}})_{k+1}\to{\mathcal{Q}}({\mathcal{A}})_{k}\end{gathered}

(126)

satisfying certain axioms. We let
\sigma_{0},\tau_{0}
be the constant maps. For
1\leq k\leq d
, we let

\begin{gathered}\sigma_{k}\begin{pmatrix}\varphi_{k+1}&:&\varphi_{k}&\rightarrow_{k}&\varphi_{k}^{\prime}\\
&&&\vdots&\\
&:&\varphi_{2}&\rightarrow_{2}&\varphi_{2}^{\prime}\\
&:&\varphi_{1}&\rightarrow_{1}&\varphi_{1}^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\varphi_{k}&:&\varphi_{k-1}&\rightarrow_{k-1}&\varphi_{k-1}^{\prime}\\
&&&\vdots&\\
&:&\varphi_{2}&\rightarrow_{2}&\varphi_{2}^{\prime}\\
&:&\varphi_{1}&\rightarrow_{1}&\varphi_{1}^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}\\
\tau_{k}\begin{pmatrix}\varphi_{k+1}&:&\varphi_{k}&\rightarrow_{k}&\varphi_{k}^{\prime}\\
&&&\vdots&\\
&:&\varphi_{2}&\rightarrow_{2}&\varphi_{2}^{\prime}\\
&:&\varphi_{1}&\rightarrow_{1}&\varphi_{1}^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\varphi_{k}^{\prime}&:&\varphi_{k-1}&\rightarrow_{k-1}&\varphi_{k-1}^{\prime}\\
&&&\vdots&\\
&:&\varphi_{2}&\rightarrow_{2}&\varphi_{2}^{\prime}\\
&:&\varphi_{1}&\rightarrow_{1}&\varphi_{1}^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}\end{gathered}

(127)

The case
k=d+1
is defined analogously by taking the appropriate sub-array. It is easy to check these definitions satisfy the “globular identities”

\begin{gathered}\sigma_{k}\circ\sigma_{k+1}=\sigma_{k}\circ\tau_{k+1}\\
\tau_{k}\circ\sigma_{k+1}=\tau_{k}\circ\tau_{k+1}\end{gathered}

(128)

so that this is indeed a globular set.

We extend
\sigma_{k}
,
\tau_{k}
to maps
\forall l\geq 0

\begin{gathered}\sigma_{k},\tau_{k}:{\mathcal{Q}}({\mathcal{A}})_{k+l+1}\to{\mathcal{Q}}({\mathcal{A}})_{k}\end{gathered}

(129)

by taking the appropriate subarrays as above.

It is useful to also define “component functions”. If

\begin{gathered}(X)=\begin{pmatrix}\varphi_{k+1}&:&\varphi_{k}&\rightarrow_{k}&\varphi_{k}^{\prime}\\
&&&\vdots&\\
&:&\varphi_{2}&\rightarrow_{2}&\varphi_{2}^{\prime}\\
&:&\varphi_{1}&\rightarrow_{1}&\varphi_{1}^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}\end{gathered}

(130)

is a
k+1
-globe, then

\begin{gathered}s_{n}(X)=\varphi_{n}\\
t_{n}(X)=\varphi_{n}^{\prime}.\end{gathered}

(131)

In this case we write, with a slight abuse of notation

\begin{gathered}(X)=\begin{pmatrix}X&:&s_{k}(X)&\rightarrow_{k}&t_{k}(X)\\
&&&\vdots&\\
&:&s_{1}(X)&\rightarrow_{1}&t_{1}(X)\end{pmatrix}\end{gathered}

(132)

where
X
represents either the QCA
\varphi_{k+1}
when
k+1\leq d+1
or the unitary
U
when
(X)
is a
d+2
-globe.

Next we will need to define compositions of
n
-globes. In particular, two
n
-globes will have
n
different composition actions depending along which direction we glue them. Compare
Section
2.2
, especially Eqs. (
170
) and (
21
) which depict the horizontal and vertical compositions of 2-globes.

Definition 22

(Globular Composition of QCA)
.

Let
k,l\geq 0
,
(X),(Y)\in{\mathcal{Q}}({\mathcal{A}})_{k+l+1}
. We say
(X)
and
(Y)
are
k
-composable as
(X)\circ_{k}(Y)
if
\sigma_{k}(X)=\tau_{k}(Y)
. Equivalently, in terms of their components,
X
and
Y
are
k
-composable iff for all
j\leq k
,

\begin{gathered}s_{j}(X)=s_{j}(Y)\\
t_{j}(X)=t_{j}(Y).\end{gathered}

(133)

Composition is defined as follows.

1.

Two 1-globes
(X)
and
(Y)
are always 0-composable since there is a unique 0-globe
\star
, and composition corresponds to QCA composition

\begin{gathered}(X)\circ_{0}(Y)=(XY).\end{gathered}

(134)

2.

Two 2-globes

\begin{gathered}(X)=(X:s_{1}(X)\to_{1}t_{1}(X))\\
(Y)=(Y:s_{1}(Y)\to_{1}t_{1}(Y))\end{gathered}

(135)

are 1-composable if
s_{1}(X)=t_{1}(Y)
. In this case, their 1-composition is (compare (
21
))

\begin{gathered}(X)\circ_{1}(Y)=(XY:s_{1}(Y)\to_{1}t_{1}(X)).\end{gathered}

(136)

Note this works because

\begin{gathered}XY:1\equiv_{1}t_{1}(X)s_{1}(X)^{-1}t_{1}(Y)s_{1}(Y)^{-1}\end{gathered}

(137)

and the 1-composability condition means
s_{1}(X)^{-1}t_{1}(Y)=1
, so
(X)\circ_{1}(Y)
is a 2-globe.

3.

In general, two
k+1
-globes
(X)
,
(Y)
are
k
-composable as
(X)\circ_{k}(Y)
if

\begin{gathered}s_{n}(X)=t_{n}(Y)\quad 1\leq n\leq{k-1}\\
s_{k}(X)=t_{k}(Y).\end{gathered}

(138)

Their composition is defined to be

\begin{gathered}(X)\circ_{k}(Y)=\begin{pmatrix}XY&:&s_{k}(Y)&\rightarrow_{k}&t_{k}(X)\\
&&&\vdots&\\
&:&s_{1}(Y)&\rightarrow_{1}&t_{1}(X)\end{pmatrix}\end{gathered}

(139)

This is a
k+1
-globe, since for
2\leq n\leq k

\begin{gathered}s_{n}(Y):1\equiv_{n-1}t_{n-1}(Y)s_{n-1}(Y)^{-1},\end{gathered}

(140)

but
t_{n-1}(Y)=t_{n-1}(X)
, so

\begin{gathered}s_{n}(Y):1\equiv_{n-1}t_{n-1}(X)s_{n-1}(Y)^{-1},\end{gathered}

(141)

and likewise for
t_{n}(X)
. At the top,

\begin{gathered}XY:1\equiv_{k}t_{k}(X)s_{k}(X)^{-1}t_{k}(Y)s_{k}(Y)^{-1},\end{gathered}

(142)

but
s_{k}(X)^{-1}t_{k}(Y)=1
by composability. Thus,
(X)\circ_{k}(Y)
defined above is a
k+1
-globe.

4.

We need to also define lower compositions between globes. Two 2-globes

\begin{gathered}(X)=(X:s_{1}(X)\to_{1}t_{1}(X))\\
(Y)=(Y:s_{1}(Y)\to_{1}t_{1}(Y))\end{gathered}

(143)

are
always
0-composable, since there is a unique 0-globe
\star
, and their 0-composition is defined to be (compare (
170
))

\begin{gathered}(X)\circ_{0}(Y)=(X\ {}^{s_{1}(X)}(Y):s_{1}(X)s_{1}(Y)\to_{1}t_{1}(X)t_{1}(Y)),\end{gathered}

(144)

where

\begin{gathered}{}^{\alpha}(\beta)=\alpha\beta\alpha^{-1}\end{gathered}

(145)

is the conjugation action.
(X)\circ_{0}(Y)
is a 2-globe because

\begin{gathered}X\ {}^{s_{1}(X)}(Y):1\equiv_{1}t_{1}(X)s_{1}(X)^{-1}s_{1}(X)t_{1}(Y)s_{1}(Y)^{-1}s_{1}(X)^{-1}\\
t_{1}(X)s_{1}(X)^{-1}s_{1}(X)t_{1}(Y)s_{1}(Y)^{-1}s_{1}(X)^{-1}=t_{1}(X)t_{1}(Y)(s_{1}(X)s_{1}(Y))^{-1}.\end{gathered}

(146)

5.

Now we express the general case. Let
(X),(Y)\in{\mathcal{Q}}({\mathcal{A}})_{k+l+1}
be
k
-composable
k+l+1
-globes,
l,k\geq 0
. We define
(X)\circ_{k}(Y)
to be

\begin{gathered}X\ {}^{s_{k+l}(X)\cdots s_{k+1}(X)}(Y)\end{gathered}

(147)

at the top level, and

\displaystyle s_{j}(X\circ_{k}Y)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}s_{j}(Y)&j\leqslant k\\
s_{j}(X)\ {}^{s_{j-1}(X)\cdots s_{k+1}(X)}(s_{j}(Y))&j>k\end{cases}

(148)

\displaystyle t_{j}(X\circ_{k}Y)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}t_{j}(X)&j\leqslant k\\
t_{j}(X)\ {}^{s_{j-1}(X)\cdots s_{k+1}(X)}(t_{j}(Y))&j>k\end{cases}

(149)

We show this defines a
k+l+1
-globe in
Proposition
14
.

Let us turn to the proof that (
147
) and (
148
) define a
k+l+1
-globe, so the compositions above are well-defined.

Proposition 14

(Composition Operations Are Well-Defined)
.

Given two
k+l+1
-globes
(X),(Y)\in{\mathcal{Q}}({\mathcal{A}})_{k+l+1}
which are
k
-composable. The data in (
147
) and (
148
) define a
k+l+1
-globe
(X)\circ_{k}(Y)
.

Proof.

We have already shown that
(X)\circ_{k}(Y)
is a
k+l+1
-globe when
l=0
. Therefore, we proceed by induction.

Suppose that the operation
\circ_{k}
is well defined on all
k
-composable pairs of
k+l
-globes. We will show that it is also well defined for composable pairs
(X),(Y)
of
k+l+1
-globes. If
(X)
and
(Y)
are
k
-composable, then
\sigma_{k+1}(X)
and
\sigma_{k+l}(Y)
are a composable pair of
k+l
-globes, as are
\tau_{k+1}(X)
and
\tau_{k+1}(Y)
. We observe that under the definition,

\begin{gathered}\sigma_{k+l}(X\circ_{k}Y)=\sigma_{k+1}(X)\circ_{k}\sigma_{k+l}(Y)\end{gathered}

(150)

and likewise for
\tau_{k+1}
. Therefore, by the inductive hypothesis
(X)\circ_{k}(Y)
satisfies

\begin{gathered}s_{n}(X\circ_{k}Y),t_{n}(X\circ_{k}Y):s_{n-1}(X\circ_{k}Y)\to_{n-1}t_{n-1}(X\circ_{k}Y)\end{gathered}

(151)

for all
n\leq k+l+1
. The only remaining thing to show is that

\begin{gathered}X\ {}^{s_{k+l}(X)\cdots s_{k+1}(X)}(Y):s_{k+l}(X\circ_{k}Y)\to_{k+l}t_{k+1}(X\circ_{k}Y)\quad(?).\end{gathered}

(152)

Recall we are interested in
l>0
, so we can write (using (
148
))

\begin{gathered}s_{k+l}(X\circ_{k}Y)=s_{j}(X)\ {}^{s_{j}(X)\cdots s_{k+1}(X)}(s_{j}(Y))\\
t_{k+l}(X\circ_{k}Y)=t_{j}(X)\ {}^{s_{j}(X)\cdots s_{k+1}(X)}(t_{j}(Y)).\end{gathered}

(153)

Now we compute

\begin{gathered}X\ {}^{s_{k+l}(X)\cdots s_{k+1}(X)}(Y):1\equiv_{k+l}t_{k+l}(X)s_{k+l}(X)^{-1}\ {}^{s_{k+l}(X)\cdots s_{k+1}(X)}(t_{k+l}(Y)s_{k+1}(Y)^{-1}).\end{gathered}

(154)

We need to check the RHS is correct:

\begin{gathered}t_{k+l}(X)s_{k+l}(X)^{-1}\ {}^{s_{k+l}(X)\cdots s_{k+1}(X)}(t_{k+l}(Y)s_{k+l}(Y)^{-1})\\
=t_{k+l}(X)\ {}^{s_{k+l-1}(X)\cdots s_{k+1}(X)}(t_{k+l}(Y)s_{k+l}(Y)^{-1})s_{k+l}(X)^{-1}\\
=t_{k+l}(X)\ {}^{s_{k+l-1}(X)\cdots s_{k+1}(X)}(t_{k+l}(Y))\left(s_{k+l}(X)\ {}^{s_{k+l-1}(X)\cdots s_{k+1}(X)}(s_{k+l}(Y))\right)^{-1},\end{gathered}

(155)

which agrees with (
148
).

∎

For computations, it is sometimes useful to express the arrays of blends in the more familiar balanced form. That is, given a
k+1
-globe
(X)
, we can define an array

\begin{gathered}\begin{Bmatrix}Xs_{k}(X)\cdots s_{1}(X)&:&s_{k}(X)s_{k-1}(X)\cdots s_{1}(X)&\equiv_{k}&t_{k}(X)s_{k-1}(X)\cdots s_{1}(X)\\
&&&\vdots&\\
&:&s_{2}(X)s_{1}(X)&\equiv_{2}&t_{2}(X)s_{1}(X)\\
&:&s_{1}(X)&\equiv_{1}&t_{1}(X)\end{Bmatrix}\end{gathered}

(156)

This is an equivalent presentation of the
k+1
-globe
(X)
, which matches the pictures in
Section
2.2
. As noted above, any two
k+1
-globes
(X)
and
(Y)
are 0-composable. Their zero composition has a simple form in this balanced presentation. Let
A_{j}(X)=s_{j}(X)\cdots s_{1}(X)
,
A_{k+1}(X)=Xs_{k}(X)\cdots s_{1}(X)
,
B_{j}(X)=t_{j}(X)s_{j-1}(X)\cdots s_{1}(X)
. The 0-composition of
(X)
and
(Y)
may be written

\begin{gathered}(X)\circ_{0}(Y)=\begin{Bmatrix}A_{k+1}(X)A_{k+1}(Y)&:&A_{k}(X)A_{k}(Y)&\equiv_{k}&B_{k}(X)B_{k}(Y)\\
&&&\vdots&\\
&:&A_{2}(X)A_{2}(Y)&\equiv_{2}&B_{2}(X)B_{2}(Y)\\
&:&A_{1}(X)A_{1}(Y)&\equiv_{1}&B_{1}(X)B_{1}(Y)\end{Bmatrix}\end{gathered}

(157)

So far we have constructed a globular set
\{{\mathcal{Q}}({\mathcal{A}})_{n}\}_{n}
with compositions defined for globes. It is easy to show each of these compositions is in fact associative and invertible, so each
{\mathcal{Q}}({\mathcal{A}})_{n}
becomes a group in
n
ways. However, one finds that these different group structures on
{\mathcal{Q}}({\mathcal{A}})_{n}
do not satisfy the “interchange law”, and so this structure
does not
define a strict
d+2
-group
23
23

23

It is easy to show, however, that the interchange law is always satisfied
up to a higher morphism
. This is because the two orders of evaluation are related by commutators of QCA, which are expressible as circuits by an argument similar to
Propositon
7
, and so both orders of evaluation are blend equivalent. All of the interchange laws involving the vertical composition of unitary cells are satisfied on-the-nose.
. However, it does define a “weak”
d+2
-group (and hence a homotopy
d+2
-type). Checking this is extremely tedious, and so we postpone it to a follow-up work
12
. Once this is checked, we can apply all of the usual methods for studying topological spaces up to homotopy to the study of
{\mathcal{Q}}({\mathcal{A}})
and lattice anomalies.

The above definitions are already sufficient for many calculations. Here is one important one:

Proposition 15
.

Let
{\mathcal{A}}
be a local algebra on
{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}
.
{\mathcal{Q}}({\mathcal{A}})
has the following homotopy groups

\begin{gathered}\pi_{0}{\mathcal{Q}}({\mathcal{A}})=\star\\
\pi_{n}{\mathcal{Q}}({\mathcal{A}})=\frac{\{\text{QCAs supported on }[a,b]^{n-1}\times{\mathbb{Z}}^{d-n+1}\text{ for some $a$, $b$}\}}{\text{blends along the $n$th axis}}\quad 1\leq n\leq d\\
\pi_{d+1}{\mathcal{Q}}({\mathcal{A}})=\frac{\text{QCAs supported on }[a,b]^{d}\text{ for some $a,b$}}{\text{local unitaries}}\\
\pi_{d+2}{\mathcal{Q}}({\mathcal{A}})=U(1)\quad\text{(with discrete topology)}\\
\pi_{>d+2}{\mathcal{Q}}({\mathcal{A}})=0.\end{gathered}

(158)

Proof.

By construction
{\mathcal{Q}}({\mathcal{A}})
is connected since it has a single 0-globe. Its homotopy groups
\pi_{n}{\mathcal{Q}}({\mathcal{A}})
may be computed
24
24

24

We are claiming here with proof to be postponed to
12
that the “obvious” definition of homotopy groups in this globular set will be the right one, applying a theorem of Ara
1
.
by considering the set of
n
-globes
(X)\in{\mathcal{Q}}({\mathcal{A}})_{n}
satisfying

\begin{gathered}s_{m}(X)=t_{m}(X)=1\quad\forall m\leq n,\end{gathered}

(159)

i.e.

\begin{gathered}(X)=\begin{pmatrix}X&:&1&\rightarrow_{n-1}&1\\
&&&\vdots&\\
&:&1&\rightarrow_{1}&1\end{pmatrix}\end{gathered}

(160)

which form a group
C_{n}
under any
k
-composition for
k<n
, which are all isomorphic. Let
1_{n}
be the identity in this group, which can be written

\begin{gathered}1_{n}=\begin{pmatrix}1&:&1&\rightarrow_{n-1}&1\\
&&&\vdots&\\
&:&1&\rightarrow_{1}&1\end{pmatrix}\end{gathered}

(161)

We consider the quotient of
C_{n}
by the (normal) subgroup
C_{n,0}
of those
n
-globes
(X)
as above for which there further exists an
n+1
-globe
(Y)
such that
\sigma_{n}(Y)=1_{n}
and
\tau_{n}(Y)=(X)
. We have

\begin{gathered}\pi_{n}{\mathcal{Q}}({\mathcal{A}})=C_{n}/C_{n,0}.\end{gathered}

(162)

Unpacking the definition, there are four cases:

1. Let
n\leq d
. An
n
-globe
(X)\in C_{n}
is determined by a QCA
X
which is supported in
[a,b]^{n-1}\times{\mathbb{Z}}^{d-n+1}
, and it is in
C_{n,0}
iff it admits a blend to the identity along the
n
th axis.

2. If
n=d+1
, an
d+1
-globe
(X)\in C_{d+1}
is determined by a QCA
X
supported in
[a,b]^{d}
for some
a,b
. It is in
C_{d+1,0}
iff it is
\text{Ad }U
for some local unitary
U
. In a tensor product bosonic Hilbert space,
\pi_{d+1}=0
. However, in a fermionic superalgebra we are interested in taking QCAs which commute with fermion parity as QCAs, meaning up to a phase, but taking only local unitaries which commute with fermion parity on the nose. Thus,
\pi_{d+1}={\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
. See also
Section
5
.

3. If
n=d+2
,
C_{d+2}
is determined by a local unitary operator
X
, but that local unitary has to satisfy
\text{Ad }X=1
as a QCA, so
X
is a phase. Therefore
\pi_{d+2}=U(1)
.

4. If
n>d+2
,
C_{n}=0
and
\pi_{n}=0
.
∎

To extend the above definitions to the case of stable
d
-QCA as in
Section
2.3
, we simply redefine

\begin{gathered}\alpha:\beta\to_{n}\gamma\end{gathered}

(163)

to mean that
\alpha,\beta,\gamma
are stable
d
-QCAs, and
\alpha
defines a blend of stable
d
-QCAs

\begin{gathered}\alpha:1\equiv_{d-m}1\quad m<n,\\
\alpha:1\equiv_{d-n}\beta\gamma.\end{gathered}

(164)

The construction also clearly extends to QCAs satisfying various conditions. For example, we can restrict all QCA to preserve a chosen product state
\psi_{0}
, as in
Section
3.1
. Or we can restrict to those commuting with an on-site symmetry as in
Section
3.3
.

4.2
Else-Nayak Revisited and Computing the Anomaly Indices

Now we want to express the obstruction-theoretic anomaly indices introduced in
Section
2.4
in terms of the
\infty
-groupoid data expressed above and describe how to obtain explicit cocycle formulas for them. Our construction gives an implementation of the proposal of Else and Nayak
16
. We will outline a construction which in principle works in every dimension, which needs to be computed once per dimension. We give calculations up to the third anomaly index, leaving an algorithmic determination of higher indices to future work.

We begin with a QCA
G
-representation
\alpha
, which defines a map

\begin{gathered}\alpha:BG\to{\mathcal{Q}}({\mathcal{A}}).\end{gathered}

(165)

We want to know whether this map is null-homotopic. In other words, we want to know if there exists a map

\begin{gathered}\beta:BG\times[0,1]\to{\mathcal{Q}}({\mathcal{A}})\end{gathered}

(166)

such that
\beta|_{BG\times 0}
is a constant map and
\beta|_{BG\times 1}=\alpha
.

To phrase this in a cellular way, we can think of the simplicial structure of
BG
giving us a cell decomposition of
BG\times[0,1]
in terms of prisms
\Delta^{n}\times[0,1]
. The map
\beta
defines a labeling of these prisms such that

1.

Every vertex is labeled by the unique object
\star
of
{\mathcal{Q}}({\mathcal{A}})
.

2.

Every
k
-simplex,
k>0
of
BG\times 0
is labeled with an identity
k
-morphism.

3.

The 1-simplices of
BG\times 1
, which are determined by elements
g\in G
, are labeled with
\alpha_{g}
.

4.

The
k
-simplices,
k>1
of
BG\times 1
are labeled with identity
k
-morphisms.

5.

The 1-simplex
\star\times[0,1]
is labeled with the identity 1-morphism.

6.

All other “interior” cells will be determined by
\beta
.

This suggest an iterative procedure to construct the null-homotopy
\beta
where we first build the skeleton
X_{0}
where only the first five types of cells are filled in, and then we add the remaining “interior”
k
-cells one level at a time, defining a sequence of spaces

\begin{gathered}X_{0}\subset X_{1}\subset\cdots\\
\bigcup_{n=0}^{\infty}X_{n}=BG\times[0,1].\end{gathered}

(167)

This gives a dual presentation (homotopy extension problem rather than homotopy lifting problem
32
) of the same obstruction sequence outlined in
Section
2.4
.

Suppose we have constructed the map
\beta_{k}:X_{k}\to{\mathcal{Q}}({\mathcal{A}})
and we now want to extend it to
X_{k+1}
. The
k+1
-cells of
X_{k+1}
are prisms
\Delta^{k}\times[0,1]
where
\Delta^{k}
is a
k
-simplex of
BG
, which is labeled by a sequence of
k
-group elements
g_{1},\ldots,g_{k}
. The boundary of the prism is topologically a
k
-sphere with a based map to
{\mathcal{Q}}({\mathcal{A}})
defined by
\beta_{k}
, and we can measure its class in
\pi_{k}{\mathcal{Q}}({\mathcal{A}})
. These combine into a group cohomology class
32

\begin{gathered}\in H^{k}(BG,\pi_{k}{\mathcal{Q}}({\mathcal{A}})),\end{gathered}

(168)

which is equal to the
k
th anomaly index
[c^{k-1}\circ\alpha^{k-1}]
defined in
Section
2.4
. We will see that this presentation of the obstruction theory closely matches the proposal of Else and Nayak
16
.

To have an explicit cocycle for the
k
th anomaly index, all we need is a way of computing the homotopy class of the boundary of a prism
\Delta^{k}\times[0,1]
with labels given by
\beta_{k}
. Homotopy groups in the globular picture appear naturally as the obstruction to filling in a hollow globe (see
Proposition
15
). Thus, we will need to evaluate hollow prisms into hollow globes. There is no canonical way of doing this, although quite general algorithms exist (see eg. “excision of extremals” algorithm in
59
). Coherence conditions imply that any two evaluations are homotopy equivalent, so any method of evaluating the diagram will give equivalent anomaly indices. Furthermore, every prism has the same shape, and is labeled by
k
abstract group elements, so we need only do one computation for each
k
to have a formula for
[\beta_{k}]
for every group
G
, every dimension
d
, and every local algebra
{\mathcal{A}}
at once.

For example, an interior
2
-cell is determined by a single element
g\in G
and must be filled in with a diagram of
{\mathcal{Q}}({\mathcal{A}})
as follows:
*
*
*
*
\beta_{g}
\scriptstyle\alpha_{g}
\scriptstyle 1
\scriptstyle 1
\scriptstyle 1

Here we draw
BG\times 1
on the top and
BG\times 0
on the bottom, so the top 1-simplex is labeled by our QCA representation
\alpha_{g}
and other 1-simplices are labeled by the identity. To be a diagram in
{\mathcal{Q}}({\mathcal{A}})
,
\beta_{g}
must be an appropriate 2-morphism. In this diagram, it goes from
1\circ 1
on the right and bottom sides to
\alpha_{g}\circ 1
on the top and left sides. It is thus equivalent to the 2-globe
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\star}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\star}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{g}}

We recognize
\beta_{g}
as defining a blend
\beta_{g}:1\equiv_{1}\alpha_{g}
. The obstruction for the existence of this blend is precisely
[\alpha_{g}]\in\pi_{1}{\mathcal{Q}}({\mathcal{A}})
. Therefore we find

\begin{gathered}=[\alpha_{g}]\in H^{1}(BG,\pi_{1}{\mathcal{Q}}({\mathcal{A}}))\end{gathered}

(169)

is the obstruction to choosing blends along the first axis for each of the QCAs
\alpha_{g}
, as argued in
Section
2.4
from the homotopy lifting problem.

Interior 3-cells take the following form

These need to be filled in with a 3-morphism
\beta_{g,h}
as shown in orange. One strategy to evaluate the diagram is to evaluate the “front” and “back” as 2-globes. Then the hollow prism is the hollow 3-globe with these 2-globes as source and target. The front of the diagram consists of

(the top triangle has the identity 2-morphism which was not shown above for simplicity, since it lies along
BG\times 1
). Since they are joined along an identity morphism, we can collapse the quadrilateral to 2-globes and evaluate them:

{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

(170)

where we used the rules for 0-composition of 2-globes. When we paste the identity morphism on top, this becomes simply

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\star}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\star}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{gh}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{g}\beta_{h}}

(171)

Meanwhile, the back of the diagram evaluates to

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\star}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\star}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{gh}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{gh}}

(172)

Thus,
\beta_{g,h}
is a 3-globe whose source is (
171
) and whose target is (
172
). We can express it as an array

\begin{gathered}(\beta_{g,h})=\begin{pmatrix}\beta_{g,h}&:&\beta_{gh}&\rightarrow_{2}&\beta_{g}\beta_{h}\\
&:&1&\rightarrow_{1}&\alpha_{gh}\end{pmatrix}\end{gathered}

(173)

In particular, it defines a blend

\begin{gathered}\beta_{g,h}:1\equiv_{2}\beta_{g}\beta_{h}\beta_{gh}^{-1}.\end{gathered}

(174)

This exists iff
\beta_{g}\beta_{h}\beta_{gh}^{-1}
is trivial in
\pi_{2}{\mathcal{Q}}({\mathcal{A}})
. Thus,

\begin{gathered}=[\beta_{g}\beta_{h}\beta_{gh}^{-1}]\in H^{2}(BG,\pi_{2}{\mathcal{Q}}({\mathcal{A}})).\end{gathered}

(175)

This invariant was recently discussed in
57
;
64
as an obstruction to on-siteability in two dimensions. Here we see how it appears from homotopy theory.

Note that this calculation does not explicitly refer to the dimension
d
of the lattice. It looks the same in all dimensions
d\geq 0
. In
d=0
, it has a special interpretation.
\beta_{g}:1\to_{1}\alpha_{g}
in
d=0
correspond to unitary operators
U_{g}
implementing
\alpha_{g}=\text{Ad}\ U_{g}
(see (
123
)). In this dimension
\pi_{2}{\mathcal{Q}}({\mathcal{A}})=U(1)
and the class
[\beta_{2}]=U_{g}U_{h}U_{gh}^{-1}\in H^{2}(BG,U(1))
represents the class of the projective representation
\alpha_{g}
.

Interior 4-cells are four dimensional prisms whose “front” and “back” faces look like the following

This prism is to be filled with the 4-cell
\beta_{g,h,k}
. As in
k=2
, we just need to evaluate the front and back of the diagram as 3-globes to get the 3rd anomaly index.

At this point, it becomes convenient to draw our prisms, which always look like
\Delta^{n}\times[0,1]
for some
n
, as simplices
\Delta^{n}
. 1-simplices are labeled by
\beta_{g}
, 2-simplices by
\beta_{g,h}
, and so on. The front and back of the 4-prism above become

\star
\star
\star
\star
\beta_{h}
\beta_{k}
\beta_{g}
\beta_{ghk}
\beta_{hk}
\Uparrow\beta_{h,k}
\Uparrow\beta_{g,hk}

(176)

\star
\star
\star
\star
\beta_{h}
\beta_{k}
\beta_{g}
\beta_{ghk}
\beta_{gh}
\Uparrow\beta_{g,h}
\Uparrow\beta_{gh,k}

(177)

We have labeled the cells with the corresponding cells from
\beta_{3}
. However, we have to express this composition in terms of globes. For example, we have to write

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

(180)

where the RHS is computing using the 0-composition of 3-globes, giving (compare (
147
))

\begin{gathered}\begin{pmatrix}1&:&\beta_{g}&\rightarrow_{2}&\beta_{g}\\
&:&1&\rightarrow_{1}&\alpha_{g}\end{pmatrix}\circ_{0}\begin{pmatrix}\beta_{h,k}&:&\beta_{hk}&\rightarrow_{2}&\beta_{hk}\\
&:&1&\rightarrow_{1}&\alpha_{hk}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}{}^{\beta_{g}}\beta_{h,k}&:&\beta_{g}\beta_{hk}&\rightarrow_{2}&\beta_{g}\beta_{hk}\\
&:&1&\rightarrow_{1}&\alpha_{ghk}\end{pmatrix}=({}^{\beta_{g}}\beta_{h,k})\end{gathered}

(181)

where
{}^{\beta_{g}}\beta_{h,k}=\beta_{g}\beta_{h,k}\beta_{g}^{-1}
. Then the 2-composition of the two 3-globes (
179
) and (
181
) gives

\begin{gathered}(\beta_{g,hk})\circ_{2}({}^{\beta_{g}}\beta_{h,k})=\begin{pmatrix}{}^{\beta_{g}}\beta_{h,k}\beta_{g,hk}&:&\beta_{g}\beta_{h}\beta_{k}&\rightarrow_{2}&\beta_{ghk}\\
&:&1&\rightarrow_{1}&\alpha_{ghk}\end{pmatrix}\end{gathered}

(182)

The evaluation of the other square is similar. The interesting piece to obtain is

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-17.22118pt}{-1.33614pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

(183)

On this side the whiskering does not modify
\beta_{g,h}
because of the asymmetry of (
147
). Combining with the other triangle, we obtain

\begin{gathered}(\beta_{g,h})\circ_{2}(\beta_{gh,k})=\begin{pmatrix}\beta_{g,h}\beta_{gh,k}&:&\beta_{g}\beta_{h}\beta_{k}&\rightarrow_{2}&\beta_{ghk}\\
&:&1&\rightarrow_{1}&\alpha_{ghk}\end{pmatrix}\end{gathered}

(184)

The 3rd anomaly index is therefore

\begin{gathered}[\beta_{3}]=[{}^{\beta_{g}}(\beta_{h,k})\beta_{g,hk}(\beta_{g,h}\beta_{gh,k})^{-1}]\in H^{3}(BG,\pi_{3}{\mathcal{Q}}({\mathcal{A}})).\end{gathered}

(185)

This gives rise to the familiar
H^{3}(BG,U(1))
Else-Nayak index when
d=1
. In higher dimensions, we see it is valued in blend equivalence classes of QCA, and defines a new obstruction to on-siteability.

5
Anomalous 1d
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
Symmetries in Fermionic Systems and the Homotopy Type of
{\mathcal{Q}}^{1}
,
{\mathcal{Q}}^{1}_{inv}

In this section, we will compute the homotopy type of
{\mathcal{Q}}^{1}
and
{\mathcal{Q}}^{1}_{inv}
for a 1d lattice of bosons and fermions. This will demonstrate the computational techniques homotopy theory makes available to us for analyzing lattice anomalies. We find some curious differences with the lore from the continuum and the Anderson dual spin cobordism. However, we show that the results of our calculations are physically sensible and give correct predictions.

It is expected based on the spin cobordism group
\Omega^{3}_{\rm spin}(B{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})={\mathbb{Z}}_{8}
that there is a
{\mathbb{Z}}_{8}
classification of
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{F}
SPTs in 2d as well as anomalies of
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{F}
symmetries in 1d fermionic systems
39
. We will show in fact that the stable lattice anomalies of QCA
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
-representations is

\begin{gathered}{\mathcal{Q}}^{1}_{f}(B{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})={\mathbb{Z}}_{4},\end{gathered}

(186)

where the subscript refers to the local Hilbert space being a two-state Fermionic Fock space. This one calculation actually allows us to completely characterize the homotopy type of
{\mathcal{Q}}^{1}_{f}
.

We will also show that

\begin{gathered}{\mathcal{Q}}^{1}_{f,inv}(B{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})={\mathbb{Z}}_{4}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}.\end{gathered}

(187)

We find in particular that
{\mathcal{Q}}^{1}_{f}
is
not
equal to the expected cobordism spectrum from
39
;
20
. However, we will show that the group above correctly classifies the SRE anomalies of symmetries
\alpha
which are
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
in the sense that their square
\alpha^{2}
admits symmetric SRE states. Therefore, this group just has a different interpretation.

The absence of the generators of
{\mathbb{Z}}_{8}
has been anticipated in several places, see eg. Appendix G of
14
and
35
. The basic reason is that such anomalous
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
symmetries should map a product state to the Kitaev chain state, a particular invertible 1d fermionic state. However, any such entangler must be a nontrivial 1d QCA since this fermionic state does not blend to a product state (it has a Majorana edge mode). The group of 1d QCA is known to be non-torsion
19
, and therefore no
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
symmetry can by a non-trivial QCA. Considering the bulk-boundary correspondence in
Section
3.3
, this also means that the corresponding 2+1d SPTs do not admit symmetric circuit disentanglers
14
.
=

Figure 9:
This figure shows the generators of a 1d local fermionic superalgebra with Majorana operators interspersed between qubit operators. The blue boxes show one possible grouping into unit cells each defining a matrix superalgebra. This local algebra is used in Section
5
to construct anomalous
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
symmetries.

Proposition 16
.

Let
{\mathcal{Q}}^{1}_{f}
be the space of stable 1-QCA on the local superalgebra built from two-state fermion Fock spaces. We have

\begin{gathered}\pi_{1}{\mathcal{Q}}^{f}_{1}={\mathbb{Z}}\\
\pi_{2}{\mathcal{Q}}^{f}_{1}={\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\\
\pi_{3}{\mathcal{Q}}^{f}_{1}=U(1)\\
\pi_{>3}{\mathcal{Q}}^{f}_{1}=0.\end{gathered}

(188)

We also compute

\begin{gathered}{\mathcal{Q}}^{f}_{1}(B{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})={\mathbb{Z}}_{4}.\end{gathered}

(189)

It follows that in general dimensions there is a non-trivial Postnikov invariant between
\pi_{d+2}{\mathcal{Q}}_{f}^{d}=U(1)
and
\pi_{d+1}{\mathcal{Q}}_{f}^{d}={\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
given by

\begin{gathered}i\circ Sq^{2}:B^{d+1}{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\to B^{d+3}U(1)_{disc}\end{gathered}

(190)

where
disc
denotes the discrete topology on
U(1)
and
i
is the inclusion map
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\to U(1)
. For
d=1
, we thus completely characterize the homotopy type
{\mathcal{Q}}^{1}_{f}
.

Proof.

The computation of the homotopy groups of
{\mathcal{Q}}^{1}_{f}
are a special case of
Proposition
15
. An interesting one in arbitrary dimensions is

\begin{gathered}\pi_{d+1}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{f}=\pi_{2}{\mathcal{Q}}^{1}_{f}=\pi_{1}Q^{0}_{f}={\mathbb{Z}}_{2}.\end{gathered}

(191)

Indeed, we require fermionic stable 0-QCA to commute with fermion parity
as QCA
. Thus, when we write them as local unitary operators they are either parity even or parity odd. If they are parity odd, then they cannot be expressed as local bosonic unitaries, and represent a non-trivial element of
\pi_{1}Q_{0}^{f}
. On the other hand, if we square such an element, it becomes parity even, and can be thus represented. Therefore,
\pi_{1}Q^{0}_{f}={\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
.

Furthermore,
\pi_{1}{\mathcal{Q}}_{1}^{f}={\mathbb{Z}}
, given by a fermionic version of the GNVW invariant
26
;
19
. The generator of this group is given by the “Majorana translation”, defined as follows. We write the Majorana generators of the Fermion algebra at site
i
as
\gamma_{i}
,
\tilde{\gamma}_{i}
. The Majorana translation acts by
\gamma_{i}\mapsto\tilde{\gamma}_{i}
,
\tilde{\gamma}_{i}\mapsto\gamma_{i+1}
(see
Fig.
10
). This has a GNVW invariant half that of a qubit translation, since the single Majorana algebra
Cl(1)
is dimension 2 and the qubit algebra
M(2,\mathbb{C})
is dimension 4.

The Postnikov tower of
{\mathcal{Q}}^{1}_{f}
thus takes the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{3}U(1)_{disc}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Q}}^{1}_{f}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{2}{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Q}}^{1}_{f,2}=B^{2}{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times B{\mathbb{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Q}}^{1}_{f,1}=B{\mathbb{Z}}}

(192)

where
{\mathcal{Q}}^{1}_{f,2}=B^{2}{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times B{\mathbb{Z}}
since
B{\mathbb{Z}}=S^{1}
is one-dimensional, and doesn’t admit non-trivial
B^{2}{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
fibrations.

The homotopy type of
{\mathcal{Q}}^{1}_{f}
is thus specified by a classifying map

\begin{gathered}B^{2}{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times B{\mathbb{Z}}\to B^{5}{\mathbb{Z}}\end{gathered}

(193)

giving the structure of the top fibration. Equivalently, the class of this fibration is given by an element of

\begin{gathered}H^{4}(B^{2}{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times B{\mathbb{Z}},U(1)_{disc})={\mathbb{Z}}_{2}.\end{gathered}

(194)

This element corresponds to

\begin{gathered}i\circ Sq^{2}:B^{2}{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\to B^{4}U(1)_{disc},\end{gathered}

(195)

where
i
is the inclusion
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\to U(1)
and
Sq^{2}
is the Steenrod square
B^{2}{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\to B^{4}{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
. Note that
B{\mathbb{Z}}
does not enter into this fibration, so

\begin{gathered}{\mathcal{Q}}^{1}_{f}=E\times B{\mathbb{Z}},\end{gathered}

(196)

where
E
sits in the fibration

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{3}U(1)_{disc}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{2}{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}}

(197)

which is yet to be determined of the two possible choices.

A short calculation shows that we can distinguish the two possibilities by studying

\begin{gathered}{\mathcal{Q}}^{1}_{f}(B{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})=\begin{cases}{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}&\text{trivial fibration}\\
{\mathbb{Z}}_{4}&\text{non-trivial fibration (correct)}\end{cases}\end{gathered}

(198)

We will show that the second holds, and therefore that the fibration is non-trivial.

The result for general dimensions will then follow. Indeed, since
{\mathcal{Q}}_{f}^{d}
is an infinite loop space, its Postnikov invariants must be stable cohomology operations.
i\circ Sq^{2}
is the only stable cohomology operation that has the right source and target, and if this Postnikov invariant is present for
d=1
it must be present for all
d\geq 1
as well.

To show
{\mathcal{Q}}^{1}_{f}(B{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})={\mathbb{Z}}_{4}
, we construct an FDQC
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
-representation, in other words an FDQC
U
(with bosonic unitary gates) satisfying
U^{2}=1
. This defines a map

\begin{gathered}U:B{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\to{\mathcal{Q}}^{1}_{f}.\end{gathered}

(199)

We will show that
U\otimes U
has a non-trivial Else-Nayak index, so

\begin{gathered}U\otimes U:B{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\to{\mathcal{Q}}^{1}_{f}\end{gathered}

(200)

factors through
B^{4}{\mathbb{Z}}\to{\mathcal{Q}}^{1}
, which is non-contractible in either case above. Thus,
U
represents a
{\mathbb{Z}}_{4}
element in the group of maps
B{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\to{\mathcal{Q}}^{1}_{f}
, showing out of the two possibilities we must have
{\mathcal{Q}}^{1}_{f}(B{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})={\mathbb{Z}}_{4}
, and the non-trivial Postnikov invariant above.

We will observe also that
U
has a truncation
\tilde{U}
such that
\tilde{U}^{2}
acts like a local fermionic operator, and cannot be represented as a local bosonic unitary. Thus,
U
already gives a nontrivial map

\begin{gathered}B{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\to B^{2}\pi_{2}{\mathcal{Q}}^{1}_{f}=B^{2}{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\end{gathered}

(201)

representing a nontrivial 2nd anomaly index (the 1st automatically vanishes because
U
is blendable, being a FDQC). Note that although in general the anomaly indices depend on choices of lifts (see Appendix
9
and
Proposition
18
), the 1st and 2nd anomaly indices are always well-defined.

We collect the construction of
U
in Example
2
. We will actually use a local algebra built from fermions and qubits (see Fig.
9
), but the qubit algebra can be regarded as a subalgebra of two fermions and the circuit
U
trivially extends with the same properties.

∎

Example 2
.

We will construct a fermionic FDQC
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
-representation with a
{\mathbb{Z}}_{4}
stable blend anomaly, demonstrating
{\mathcal{Q}}^{1}_{f}(B{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})={\mathbb{Z}}_{4}
. There are many related constructions in the literature, especially the works of
14
;
60
, which constructed circuit disentanglers for the corresponding 2+1d
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
SPT. Using the bulk-boundary correspondence (see
Section
3.3
), one can produce corresponding QCA representations in 1d. Here we give a simple and direct construction in terms of a finite depth circuit.

Let us consider a 1d lattice
{\mathbb{Z}}
with bosonic qubits at each odd site, with algebra generators
X_{2n+1}
,
Z_{2n+1}
, and a single Majorana operator
\gamma_{2n}
at each even site (see Fig.
9
).
Let us define the “controlled parity gate”

\begin{gathered}CP_{2n+1}=\exp\left(i\pi\left(\frac{1-Z_{2n+1}}{2}\right)\left(\frac{1-i\gamma_{2n}\gamma_{2n+2}}{2}\right)\right).\end{gathered}

(202)

In the
Z_{2n+1}
basis, this operator acts as the identity if
Z_{2n+1}=1
, and acts as
i\gamma_{2n}\gamma_{2n+2}
if
Z_{2n+1}=-1
. It satisfies

\begin{gathered}CP_{2n+1}^{2}=1\\
CP_{2n+1}X_{2n+1}CP_{2n+1}=i\gamma_{2n}X_{2n+1}\gamma_{2n+2}\\
CP_{2n+1}\gamma_{2n}CP_{2n+1}=\gamma_{2n}Z_{2n+1}\\
CP_{2n+1}\gamma_{2n+2}CP_{2n+1}=Z_{2n+1}\gamma_{2n+2}\\
CP_{2n+1}CP_{2n+3}CP_{2n+1}CP_{2n+3}=\exp\left(i\pi\left(\frac{1-Z_{2n+1}}{2}\right)\left(\frac{1-Z_{2n+3}}{2}\right)\right)=CZ_{2n+1}.\end{gathered}

(203)

Other commutation relations with generators are trivial. The last relation can be checked in the
Z_{2n+1}
,
Z_{2n+3}
basis. If
Z_{2n+1}=1
or
Z_{2n+3}=1
, then the operators commute, since one acts as the identity. However, if
Z_{2n+1}=Z_{2n+3}=-1
, they act as
i\gamma_{2n}\gamma_{2n+2}
and
i\gamma_{2n+2}\gamma_{2n+4}
, which anti-commute.

Now let us consider the circuit

\begin{gathered}C=\prod_{n}CP_{4n+3}\prod_{n}CP_{4n+1}.\end{gathered}

(204)

We have

\begin{gathered}C^{2}=\prod_{n}CZ_{2n+1}=\prod_{n}e^{(-1)^{n}\frac{i\pi}{4}Z_{2n+1}Z_{2n+3}}\qquad\text{(up to phases)}\\
C^{\dagger}X_{4n+1}C=i\gamma_{4n}X_{4n+1}\gamma_{4n+2}\\
C^{\dagger}X_{4n+3}C=iZ_{4n+1}\gamma_{4n+2}X_{4n+3}\gamma_{4n+4}Z_{4n+5}\\
C^{\dagger}\prod_{n}X_{2n}C=\prod_{n}X_{2n}\qquad\text{(up to phases)}.\end{gathered}

(205)

Finally we construct the depth four circuit

\begin{gathered}U=\left(\prod_{n}e^{(-1)^{n}i\frac{\pi}{8}Z_{2n+1}Z_{2n+3}}\right)C\left(\prod_{n}X_{2n+1}\right)\\
=\prod_{n}e^{(-1)^{n}i\frac{\pi}{8}Z_{2n+1}Z_{2n+3}}\prod_{n}CP_{4n+3}\prod_{n}CP_{4n+1}\prod_{n}X_{2n+1}.\end{gathered}

(206)

A small computation shows
U^{2}=1
(up to phases), so
U
defines an FDQC
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
-representation on this fermionic algebra.

We wish to compute the anomaly indices of
U
. First, we define a truncation of the circuit

\begin{gathered}\tilde{U}=\prod_{n\geq 0}e^{(-1)^{n}i\frac{\pi}{8}Z_{2n+1}Z_{2n+3}}\prod_{n\geq 0}CP_{4n+3}\prod_{n\geq 0}CP_{4n+1}\prod_{n\geq 0}X_{2n+1}.\end{gathered}

(207)

A short calculation shows

\begin{gathered}\tilde{U}^{2}=\gamma_{0}e^{\frac{i\pi}{4}Z_{1}}\qquad\text{(up to phases)}.\end{gathered}

(208)

Since this is a fermionic operator,
\tilde{U}^{2}
is not realizable as a local unitary. Therefore, we have encountered a non-trivial anomaly index.

Suppose now we take two tensor copies of our 1d lattice, call the layers
A
and
B
, and consider the symmetry
U\otimes U=U_{A}U_{B}
. We will have

\begin{gathered}(\tilde{U}_{A}\tilde{U}_{B})^{2}=\gamma_{A,0}e^{\frac{i\pi}{4}Z_{A,1}}\gamma_{B,0}e^{\frac{i\pi}{4}Z_{B,1}}\qquad\text{(up to phases)}.\end{gathered}

(209)

Unlike the case with a single layer, now the RHS is representable by the local unitary operator

\begin{gathered}N=\gamma_{A,0}e^{\frac{i\pi}{4}Z_{A,1}}\gamma_{B,0}e^{\frac{i\pi}{4}Z_{B,1}}.\end{gathered}

(210)

We can proceed to compute the Else-Nayak index
\omega=\beta_{3}
:

\begin{gathered}e^{i\omega(1,1,1)}=N\left((\tilde{U}_{A}\tilde{U}_{B})N(\tilde{U}_{A}\tilde{U}_{B})^{\dagger}\right)^{\dagger}\end{gathered}

(211)

(see (
185
)). We find

\begin{gathered}\tilde{U}\gamma_{0}\tilde{U}^{\dagger}=\gamma_{0}Z,\end{gathered}

(212)

so

\begin{gathered}(\tilde{U}_{A}\tilde{U}_{B})N(\tilde{U}_{A}\tilde{U}_{B})^{\dagger}=\gamma_{A,0}e^{-\frac{i\pi}{4}Z_{A,1}}Z_{A,1}\gamma_{B,0}e^{-\frac{i\pi}{4}Z_{B,1}}Z_{B,1},\end{gathered}

(213)

so

\begin{gathered}e^{i\omega(1,1,1)}=\gamma_{A,0}^{2}e^{\frac{i\pi}{2}Z_{A,1}}Z_{A,1}\gamma_{B,0}^{2}e^{\frac{i\pi}{2}Z_{B,1}}Z_{B,1}=iZ_{A,1}^{2}iZ_{B,1}^{2}=-1.\end{gathered}

(214)

Thus, we find the non-trivial Else-Nayak index
[\omega]\in H^{3}(B{\mathbb{Z}}_{2},U(1))
for two tensor copies of the fermionic FDQC
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
-representation
U
.

Proposition 17
.

Let
{\mathcal{Q}}^{1}_{f,inv}
be the space of FDQC-invertible stable
1
-states in the same fermionic algebra. We have

\begin{gathered}\pi_{0}{\mathcal{Q}}^{1}_{f,inv}={\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\\
\pi_{1}{\mathcal{Q}}^{1}_{f,inv}={\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\\
\pi_{2}{\mathcal{Q}}^{1}_{f,inv}=U(1)\\
\pi_{>2}{\mathcal{Q}}^{1}_{f,inv}=0.\end{gathered}

(215)

We also compute

\begin{gathered}{\mathcal{Q}}^{1}_{f,inv}(B{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})={\mathbb{Z}}_{4}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}.\end{gathered}

(216)

Proof.

The homotopy groups follow from the long exact sequence (
73
). This long exact sequence is convenient to represent in an array with connecting maps going between rows from
\pi_{n}{\mathcal{Q}}^{1}_{f,inv}\to\pi_{n}{\mathcal{Q}}^{1}_{f,\psi_{0}}
. In this case, we have

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n+1}{\mathcal{Q}}^{1}_{f,\psi_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n+1}{\mathcal{Q}}^{1}_{f}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n}{\mathcal{Q}}^{1}_{f,inv}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 3}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 2}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U(1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U(1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}}

(217)

The
n=3
row is zero because
{\mathcal{Q}}^{1}_{f,\psi_{0}}
and
{\mathcal{Q}}^{1}_{f}
are homotopy 2-types by construction.

For
n=2
, consider first
\pi_{3}{\mathcal{Q}}^{1}_{f,\psi_{0}}
. These correspond to local unitary operators
U
with
\text{Ad}\ U=1
satisfying
U\psi_{0}=\psi_{0}
. Such
U
must be a phase operator since
\text{Ad}\ U=1
and therefore must in fact have
U=1
to fix the product state.

For
n=1
, consider
\pi_{2}{\mathcal{Q}}^{1}_{f,\psi_{0}}=\pi_{1}{\mathcal{Q}}^{0}_{f,\psi_{0}}
. These are automorphisms
\alpha
of a superalgebra commuting with the fermion parity and fixing
\psi_{0}
. Since
\psi_{0}
has definite fermion parity, such an
\alpha
must be representable by a bosonic unitary operator, and thus
\pi_{1}{\mathcal{Q}}^{0}_{f,\psi_{0}}=0
. (Compare
Proposition
15
). The previous row now completely follows by exactness.

Finally, for the short exact sequence of
n=0
, the Majorana translation
\alpha
(see
Fig.
10
) the generator of the group
{\mathcal{Q}}^{1}_{f}

26
;
19
. It takes the product state
\psi_{0}
to the Kitaev chain
\psi_{Kitaev}=\psi_{0}\circ\alpha
, which does not admit a blend to a product state, so
\alpha
mapsto a non-trivial element of
\pi_{0}{\mathcal{Q}}^{1}_{f,inv}
.

It follows from exactness that
\alpha
cannot fix an SRE state. There is also a nice argument for this directly from the non-triviality of
\psi_{Kitaev}
. Suppose towards a contradiction that
\alpha
fixes an SRE state, meaning we have
\psi_{0}\circ C\circ\alpha=\psi_{0}\circ C
for some FDQC
C
. Then we have
\psi_{Kitaev}=\psi_{0}\circ\alpha=\psi_{0}\circ(C\alpha^{-1}C^{-1}\alpha)
, but
(C\alpha^{-1}C^{-1}\alpha)
is a circuit, contradicting the LRE of
\psi_{Kitaev}
.

On the other hand, it is easy to see
\alpha^{2}
fixes the product state (see
Fig.
10
). Therefore
\alpha^{2}
generates the kernel of the map
\psi_{0}:\pi_{1}{\mathcal{Q}}^{1}_{f}\to\pi_{0}{\mathcal{Q}}^{1}_{f,inv}
. The bottom row now follows.

Figure 10:
(a)
The “Majorana translation” QCA
\alpha
, acting on a 1d lattice with a Majorana operator
\gamma_{n}
at each site
n
. The QCA acts by
\gamma_{n}\mapsto\gamma_{n+1}
. To have a (super)tensor product Hilbert space, we can group the Majoranas in pairs, say
\gamma_{2n},\gamma_{2n+1}
. After doing so, one can define a product state
\psi_{0}
as the simultaneous eigenvector of all
i\gamma_{2n}\gamma_{2n+1}=1
(blue bonds). Applying the Majorana translation
\alpha
to this state yields the famous Kitaev chain state
41
, a long range entangled 1d state of fermions. However, applying
\alpha^{2}
fixes the state
\psi_{0}
.
(b)
A product state fixed by
\alpha\otimes\alpha
, which shows that the anomaly of
\alpha
is order 2.

From the long exact sequence (
83
), we find that the map
SRE:{\mathcal{Q}}^{1}_{f}(B{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})={\mathbb{Z}}_{4}\to\Omega^{1}_{f,inv}(B{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})
is injective since
{\mathcal{Q}}^{1}_{f,\psi_{0}}=B{\mathbb{Z}}
so
{\mathcal{Q}}^{1}_{f,\psi_{0}}(B{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})=0
. From the Atiyah-Hirzebruch spectral sequence using the homotopy groups computed above, we further learn that
{\mathcal{Q}}^{1}_{f,inv}(B{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})
is one of

\begin{gathered}{\mathbb{Z}}_{4},\qquad{\mathbb{Z}}_{4}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2},\quad\text{or}\quad{\mathbb{Z}}_{8},\end{gathered}

(218)

where the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{4}
subgroup in each case is the image of
{\mathcal{Q}}^{1}_{f}(B{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})
.

The Majorana translation
\alpha
defines a QCA such that
\alpha^{2}
fixes the product state
\psi_{0}
. It thus defines a map

\begin{gathered}\alpha:B{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\to B{\mathcal{Q}}^{1}_{f,inv}\end{gathered}

(219)

which is not null-homotopic by the first anomaly index, since
[\alpha]\neq 0\in\pi_{0}{\mathcal{Q}}^{1}_{f,inv}
, which we argued above. However,
\alpha\otimes\alpha
can be easily shown to be null-homotopic (see
Fig.
10
). Therefore, the homotopy class
[\alpha]\in{\mathcal{Q}}^{1}_{f}(B{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})
is a non-zero, 2-torsion element. It is also not in the image of the map
SRE
, since the generator of that
{\mathbb{Z}}_{4}
has trivial first anomaly index, being given by an FDQC. Therefore,
{\mathcal{Q}}^{1}_{f,inv}(B{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})={\mathbb{Z}}_{4}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
.
∎

We see from this proof that although we did not obtain the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{8}
anomaly group expected from the continuum, the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
factor of
{\mathcal{Q}}^{1}_{f,inv}(B{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})={\mathbb{Z}}_{4}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
has a natural interpretation on the lattice: it is the anomaly of a symmetry which defines a
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
group
up to a QCA fixing a product state
.

It is interesting to consider how this is consistent with the continuum reasoning. For instance, it is known that if we take the Hamiltonian

\begin{gathered}H=\sum_{n}i\gamma_{n}\gamma_{n+1},\end{gathered}

(220)

this is symmetric under the Majorana translation
\alpha
and has a single gapless Majorana fermion in the IR. From the point of view of the continuum QFT,
\alpha
becomes the chiral parity of this Majorana fermion (for some recent perspectives on this, see
56
). This anomaly represents the generator of the cobordism group
\tilde{\Omega}^{3}_{\rm Spin}(B{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})={\mathbb{Z}}_{8}
(using reduced cohomology here because there is no gravitational anomaly).

If we take two copies of this system, the emanant symmetry is still a
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
symmetry with a non-trivial anomaly
\nu=2
mod 8 in
{\mathbb{Z}}_{8}
. However, now we can add to this system the trivial symmetric Hamiltonian in
Figure
10
. This will eventually drive the system into a trivial phase, which seems paradoxical. However, there is no paradox, because as this perturbation is turned on, the emanant
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
symmetry of the two Majorana fermions, which may be considered as a single Dirac, actually becomes a
{\mathbb{Z}}
subgroup of a chiral
U(1)
symmetry.

We can see what happens to the anomaly when the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
symmetry is lifted to
{\mathbb{Z}}
by pulling back along the quotient map
B{\mathbb{Z}}\to B{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
:

\begin{gathered}\tilde{\Omega}^{3}_{\rm Spin}(B{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})\to\tilde{\Omega}^{3}_{\rm Spin}(B{\mathbb{Z}})={\mathbb{Z}}_{2}.\end{gathered}

(221)

Thus, the anomalies where
\nu
is even all become trivial, and a trivial symmetric phase is now permitted.

Indeed, as the strength of the perturbation is increased, the symmetry emanating from
\alpha
deforms inside the chiral
U(1)
symmetry of the Dirac fermion until it reaches a point where a symmetric mass term appears, gapping the system into a trivial symmetric phase. The same mechanism was discussed for translations acting as an emanant anomalous
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
symmetry of bosons in
50
.

6
Outlook

In this work, we have made some first steps towards a topological theory of anomalies on the lattice. We have defined blend anomalies which are obstructions to on-siteability/gauging, and SRE anomalies which are obstructions to having a symmetric SRE state. Both types of anomalies are phrased in terms of a homotopy class of a map to either a classifying space of QCAs or a classifying space of FDQC-invertible states. We have shown in the stable setting these classifying spaces form
\Omega
-spectra, and are in fact closely related by a cofiber sequence. We have shown how to use Else-Nayak-style methods and obstruction theory to calculate the anomalies on the lattice, and have used homotopy theory to compute some of the relevant classifications, uncovering some intriguing (although resolvable) tension with the theory of ’t Hooft anomalies of QFT.

There are several important problems which remain open. One is how to include invertible states with a non-zero correlation length, such as Chern insulators, which cannot be FDQC-invertible. One approach would be to study approximate QCA, or QCA with tails, and proceed as we have in
Section
2.3
. One needs only a suitable notion of blend of such approximate QCA. A difficulty we foresee, which is one reason we have not pursued it here, is that one cannot restrict to finite blend intervals once there are tails. A blend of approximate QCA should more smoothly interpolate from one to another. In particular, a blend from the identity to itself is not a strictly lower dimensional QCA, as we needed to have the
\Omega
-spectrum property, but instead will spread out completely in the extra dimension, but become more and more like the identity from far away. Should we accept this as natural? When can we get something strictly lower-dimensional?

A second important problem concerns a pathology of our blend equivalence, which is defined with respect to a particular axis. If we consider 2d QCA up to blend equivalence along the 1st axis, one can show there are continuum many distinct equivalence classes, since we may produce any rational or irrational density of translations along our axis, spaced in a particular way along the other axis. If there are non-torsion invertible states like Chern insulators, we can do the same thing. We can also form concentric spheres of these states or QCA, which do not admit a blend along any axis, and again seem to have continuum many density parameters. A related problem is to define an equivalence relation such that two invertible states which differ by a rotation are equivalent, which is not obviously true for blend equivalence. For recent progress in this last direction, see
58
.

A third class of problems concerns whether our anomalies are complete. They are defined in terms of homotopies, so when the anomaly vanishes, we may obtain a null-homotopy of the corresponding map. We would like to know whether this null-homotopy can do something useful for us. For example, if the blend anomaly vanishes, does that mean we can gauge (this is probably too naive)? Or if the SRE anomaly vanishes, does that mean a symmetric SRE state really exists? These are crucial problems for example in constructing chiral gauge theories and realizing symmetric mass generation on the lattice.

Finally, there are questions relating these lattice anomalies to the continuum. We have said some words about this at the end of
Section
5
in regards to a particular interesting example. But there are many interesting general questions. For instance, is there a map
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\star}_{{\mathcal{H}},inv}
to the cobordism spectrum? Are
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\star}_{{\mathcal{H}},inv}
or
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\star}_{{\mathcal{H}}}
spectra of invertible TQFTs of some kind?

We are very grateful to Lei Gioia, Corey Jones, Anton Kapustin, Alexei Kitaev, Max Metlitski, Daniel Ranard, Nikita Sopenko, Ruben Verresen, and Dominic Williamson for enlightening discussions and collaborations on related works. RT is supported by the Bhaumik Presidential Term Chair. RG acknowledges support by the Mani L. Bhaumik Institute for Theoretical Physics and support from the Simons Center for Geometry and Physics, Stony Brook University at which some of the research for this paper was performed.

7
Proof of
Theorem
9
on the spectrum of FDQC-invertible states

Proof.

For the abelian group structure on blend equivalence classes of FDQC-invertible states is given by tensor product

\begin{gathered}+[\psi^{\prime}]=[\psi\otimes\psi^{\prime}],\end{gathered}

(222)

which makes sense for
\psi
and
\psi^{\prime}
which have disjoint domains. We can always use a layer-shifting blend as in
Figure
5
to move the domain of
\psi
in its blend equivalence class to ensure this is the case. This operation does not depend on the arrangement of the domains of
\psi
and
\psi^{\prime}
up to blending of
\psi\otimes\psi^{\prime}
. Furthermore, if we have a blend of
\psi
, we can make the domain of this blend disjoint from
D(\psi^{\prime})
and thus obtain a blend of
\psi\otimes\psi^{\prime}
. Therefore, the operation above is well-defined.

The operation is also clearly commutative, associative, and has
[\bar{\psi}_{0}]
as the identity, where
\bar{\psi}_{0}
is the product state obtained by taking tensor products of
\psi_{0}
over any valid domain. Inverses are given by the state
\psi^{\text{rev}}
reflected along the
d
th axis, with blend
\psi\otimes\psi^{\text{rev}}
to
\bar{\psi}_{0}
given by the “folding blend”, as in
Figure
11
.

Let
\text{Inv}^{d}
denote this group. We want to show it is isomorphic to a certain homotopy group of the cofiber spectrum.

Figure 11:
A folding blend of FDQC-invertible stable states gives a blend from
\psi\otimes\psi^{\text{rev}}
to
\bar{\psi}_{0}
, (compare
Figure
6
). This shows that
\psi^{\text{rev}}
is an inverse to
\psi
in the group of blend-equivalence classes of FDQC-invertible states.

The homotopy group of interest can be computed as a stable relative homotopy group

\begin{gathered}\pi_{0}({\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{{\mathcal{H}},inv})=\lim_{r\to\infty}\pi_{r+1}(\Omega_{\star}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d+r+1}_{\mathcal{H}},\Omega_{\star}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d+r+1}_{{\mathcal{H}},\psi_{0}},1).\end{gathered}

(223)

Unpacking this,
\pi_{r+1}(\Omega_{\star}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d+r+1}_{\mathcal{H}},\Omega_{\star}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d+r+1}_{{\mathcal{H}},\psi_{0}},1)
is the group of equivalence classes of
r+1
-globes of stable
d+r+1
-QCA (see
Proposition
15
, the same caveats apply here), which are expressed as an array of blends (using the balanced presentation (
157
))

\begin{gathered}\left\{\begin{array}[]{r c l c l}\varphi_{r+1}&\mathrel{:}&\varphi_{r}&\equiv_{d+1}&\varphi_{r}^{\prime}\\
&&\vdots&&\\
&\mathrel{:}&\varphi_{2}&\equiv_{d+r}&\varphi_{2}^{\prime}\\
&\mathrel{:}&\varphi_{1}&\equiv_{d+r+1}&1\end{array}\right\}\end{gathered}

(224)

where
\varphi_{1}
is a stable
d+r+1
-QCA which fixes
\bar{\psi}_{0}
, and for
2\leq n\leq r
,
\varphi_{n},\varphi_{n}^{\prime}
are blends of stable
d+r+1
-QCA from
\varphi_{n-1}
to
\varphi_{n-1}^{\prime}
along the
d+r+2-n
th axis, all fixing
\bar{\psi}_{0}
, and finally
\varphi_{r+1}:\varphi_{r}\equiv_{d+1}\varphi_{r}^{\prime}
is just required to be a blend along the
d+1
st axis. Note that
\varphi_{r+1}
fixes the product state outside of

\begin{gathered}{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}\times[-w,w]^{l}\times 0\times 0\times\cdots\end{gathered}

(225)

for some
w,l
. Furthermore,
\varphi_{r+1}
acts only in a neighborhood of
{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{\leq 0}^{r+1}
because it starts with a blend to the identity.

The
r+1
-globes of this form can be checked to be closed under 0-composition of
r+1
-blends, defined by element-wise composition in the array (see (
157
), the homotopy group can be constructed using composition along any direction, by the usual Eckman-Hilton argument, the 0-composition is just the easiest to work with in the balanced presentation). In the relative homotopy group, these
r+1
-globes are considered up to blend equivalence along the
d
th axis and up to composition with an
r+1
-globe of the same type where moreover
\varphi_{r+1}
fixes the product state
\bar{\psi}_{0}
everywhere.

For the rest of the proof we refer to these
r+1
-globes as “
r
-blends”.

To compute the stable relative homotopy group, we can consider any such
r
-blend as an
r+1
-blend of stable
d+r+2
QCA

\begin{gathered}\left\{\begin{array}[]{r c l c l}\varphi_{r+1}&\mathrel{:}&\varphi_{r}&\equiv_{d+1}&\varphi_{r}^{\prime}\\
&&\vdots&&\\
&\mathrel{:}&\varphi_{2}&\equiv_{d+r}&\varphi_{2}^{\prime}\\
&\mathrel{:}&\varphi_{1}&\equiv_{d+r+1}&1\end{array}\right\}\sim\left\{\begin{array}[]{r c l c l}\varphi_{r+1}&\mathrel{:}&\varphi_{r}&\equiv_{d+1}&\varphi_{r}^{\prime}\\
&&\vdots&&\\
&\mathrel{:}&\varphi_{2}&\equiv_{d+r}&\varphi_{2}^{\prime}\\
&\mathrel{:}&\varphi_{1}&\equiv_{d+r+1}&1\\
&\mathrel{:}&1&\equiv_{d+r+2}&1\end{array}\right\}\end{gathered}

(226)

and this way take the limit
r\to\infty
.

Given such an
r
-blend, we can apply
\varphi_{r+1}
to the state
\bar{\psi}_{0}
and produce a stable
d
-state

\begin{gathered}\psi=\bar{\psi}_{0}\circ\varphi_{r+1}\end{gathered}

(227)

whose domain is

\begin{gathered}{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}\times[-w,w]^{l}\times 0\times\cdots.\end{gathered}

(228)

Let us show this state is FDQC-invertible. If we follow the argument of
Proposition
7
, we can construct an FDQC
\varphi_{r+1}\otimes\varphi_{r+1}^{-1}
which creates

\begin{gathered}\psi\otimes\psi^{\prime},\end{gathered}

(229)

where
\psi^{\prime}=\bar{\psi}_{0}\circ\varphi_{r+1}^{-1}
. This is not quite satisfactory, because
\varphi_{r+1}\otimes\varphi_{r+1}^{-1}
acts in a
d+r+1
-dimensional space. However, since it is a circuit, we can truncate it on a large finite
d
-dimensional neighborhood of
{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}
. This creates

\begin{gathered}\psi\otimes\psi^{\prime}\otimes\psi_{\partial},\end{gathered}

(230)

where
\psi_{\partial}
is supported near the boundary of this neighborhood, which is a
d
-dimensional tube. This is shown in
Figure
12
. Thus,
\psi
is FDQC-invertible as a stable
d
-state.

Figure 12:
To adapt the argument of
Proposition
7
and
Corollary
2
to the stable case, we note that
\varphi_{r+1}\otimes\varphi_{r+1}
is an FDQC in
d+r+1
dimensions, and can be arbitrarily truncated. Truncating it in a tubular neighborhood, large enough so that its boundary is causally disconnected from the region where
\psi\otimes\psi^{\prime}
is created, we obtain
\psi\otimes\psi^{\prime}\otimes\psi_{\partial}
, where
\psi_{\partial}
is a state supported near the boundary of the tube. This boundary is
d
-dimensional and so
\psi_{\partial}
can be regarded as a stable
d
-state. The FDQC-inverse of
\psi
is therefore
\psi^{\prime}\otimes\psi_{\partial}
.

If we have a blend between two
r
-blends, we obtain an FDQC-invertible blend between the two FDQC-invertible states. Furthermore, if we compose two blends, which can always first be blended so to have disjoint domains, the states we obtain by their composition is the tensor product. Thus, we obtain a group homomorphism

\begin{gathered}f_{r}:\pi_{r+1}(\Omega_{\star}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d+r+1}_{\mathcal{H}},\Omega_{\star}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d+r+1}_{{\mathcal{H}},\psi_{0}},1)\to\text{Inv}^{d}.\end{gathered}

(231)

This map is clearly compatible with the stabilization from
r
-blends to
r+1
-blends so we also get a group homomorphism

\begin{gathered}f:\lim_{r\to\infty}\pi_{r+1}(\Omega_{\star}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d+r+1}_{\mathcal{H}},\Omega_{\star}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d+r+1}_{{\mathcal{H}},\psi_{0}},1)\to\text{Inv}^{d}.\end{gathered}

(232)

We want to show
f
is an isomorphism.

We first show
f
is surjective. Given an FDQC-invertible state
\psi
, we can construct the swindle circuit as in
Proposition
8
. Actually we can make a two-sided swindle circuit

\begin{gathered}S=\prod_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}D_{2n+1}\prod_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}C_{2n}\end{gathered}

(233)

as in the notation of the proposition. This circuit
S
fixes
\bar{\psi}_{0}
everywhere. We can choose a truncation
S_{\leq 0}
which creates
\psi
at its boundary. We can regard this as a 1-blend

\begin{gathered}\left\{\begin{array}[]{r c l c l}S_{\leq 0}&\mathrel{:}&S&\equiv_{d+1}&1\end{array}\right\}\end{gathered}

(234)

which is of the type we have been considering. If we apply
f
to this 1-blend, we get
\psi
be construction, so
f
is surjective.

Figure 13:
Using a corner variant of the swindle circuit of
Proposition
8
, we can produce FDQC-invertible states along right angled regions using circuits acting on corner regions as shown, where the first layer of the swindle circuit is shown in green and the second layer is shown in blue. (For simplicity we have just depicted the green and blue circuits as two-body gates to show the connectivity.) All sites in the interior of the corner return to the product state
\bar{\psi}_{0}
, while sites on the boundary produce the desired FDQC-invertible state. For the proof of the theorem, we want to apply this construction to our blend
\psi^{\prime}
of FDQC-invertible states to produce a circuit
S_{NE}
as shown. This circuit acts on a finite-thickening of
{\mathbb{Z}}^{d-1}
times the NE corner region in the
x_{d},x_{d+1}
plane, such that along the thickened
d
-dimensional half space
{\mathbb{Z}}^{d-1}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
it produces the state
\psi
(blue), except for a finite neighborhood of
{\mathbb{Z}}^{d-1}
where the blend region of
\psi^{\prime}
is (orange), and elsewhere it produces
\bar{\psi}_{0}
(gray).

To prove injectivity, suppose we have an
r
-blend as above such that the resulting state
\psi=\bar{\psi}_{0}\circ\varphi_{r+1}
blends to
\bar{\psi}_{0}
along the
d
th axis by an another FDQC-invertible stable
d
-state state
\psi^{\prime}
. We want to show that
\varphi_{r+1}
is equivalent to the identity.

Since
\psi^{\prime}
is FDQC-invertible, it has a swindle circuit
S
as in
Proposition
8
which acts on a neighborhood of the half-space
{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
. Consider the
x_{d},x_{d+1}
-plane. The half space
{\mathbb{Z}}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
in this plane can be mapped bijectively to the NE corner
{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
by mapping

\begin{gathered}f(x_{d},x_{d+1})=\begin{cases}(x_{d}+x_{d+1},x_{d+1})&x_{d}\geq 0\\
(x_{d+1},-x_{d}+x_{d+1})&x_{d}\leq 0\end{cases}\end{gathered}

(235)

This mapping can be visualized geometrically as two shear transformations joined along the line
x_{d+1}=x_{d}
. Therefore,
d(f(\vec{x}),f(\vec{y}))\leq Cd(\vec{x},\vec{y})
, where
C
is a constant independent of
\vec{x}
and
\vec{y}
and
d
is the Euclidean distance. Therefore, we obtain another FDQC
f(S)
(by permuting sites according to
