) which produces the state
\psi^{\prime}
along the
x_{d+1}=0
space, which is a right angle

\begin{gathered}{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}\times 0\cup 0\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}.\end{gathered}

(236)

We arrange this so as in
Figure
13
we obtain the “corner swindle” circuit
S_{NE}
that produces a state
\psi^{\prime}
along
{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}\times 0
and elsewhere yields the product state.

We now consider truncating
S_{NE}
at a finite but large enough
x_{d+1}
. This produces the blended state
\psi^{\prime}
on the lower edge and another state
\psi^{\prime\prime}
on its upper edge. These states can be regarded as inverses. Working as in
Proposition
8
, we now build a swindle out of the truncated
S_{NE}
to obtain a circuit
S_{NE}^{\prime}
acting on the corner space which produces
\psi^{\prime}
on its boundary.

The advantage of
S_{NE}^{\prime}
now is that we can regard it as a truncation of a circuit
S_{E}
acting in the whole right half space
x_{d}\geq 0
which preserves
\bar{\psi}_{0}
everywhere by construction. Thus, we may regard
S_{NE}^{\prime}
as an
r
-blend. Furthermore,
S_{NE}^{\prime}
is equivalent to a trivial
r
-blend, since it admits a blend to the identity along the
d
th axis (the blend is
S_{NE}^{\prime}
itself). Thus,

\begin{gathered}\varphi_{r+1}^{\prime}=(S_{NE}^{\prime})^{-1}\circ\varphi_{r+1}\sim\varphi_{r+1}\end{gathered}

(237)

(equivalence as
r
-blends).

The upshot of this construction is that
\varphi_{r+1}^{\prime}
is an equivalent
r
-blend which creates a state
\psi^{\prime\prime}
along the thickened half-space

\begin{gathered}{\mathbb{Z}}^{d-1}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{\leq 0}\times[-w^{\prime},w^{\prime}]^{l^{\prime}}\end{gathered}

(238)

and elsewhere fixes
\bar{\psi}_{0}
.

Figure 14:
The composition
(S_{NE}^{\prime})^{-1}\circ\varphi_{r+1}=\varphi_{r+1}^{\prime}
produces an
r
-blend which along a thickened half space
H={\mathbb{Z}}^{d-1}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{\leq 0}
we obtain
\psi
(blue), except for a bounded neighborhood of
{\mathbb{Z}}^{d-1}
(orange), and elsewhere we obtain
\psi_{0}
. Utilizing a free direction in the infinite dimensional space
{\mathbb{Z}}^{\omega}
, we fold this QCA like a pastry (similar to
Figure
6
), to obtain a new QCA
P_{NW}
which produces the same state as
\varphi_{r+1}^{\prime}
but acts only in a neighborhood of
{\mathbb{Z}}^{d-1}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{\leq 0}^{r+2}
. (Note that
\varphi_{r+1}^{\prime}
acts only in a neighborhood of
{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{\leq 0}^{r+1}
because it starts with a 1-blend to the identity.)

For the next step we consider folding
\varphi_{r+1}^{\prime}
over a codimension 1 hyperplane at fixed
x_{d}
, using an unused direction, to produce an
r
-blend
P_{NW}
as in
Figure
14
. All the maps in the array specifying the
r
-blend are likewise folded, and so we obtain an
r
-blend among blends all of which fix
\bar{\psi}_{0}
. Furthermore,
P_{NW}
is equivalent to a trivial
r
-blend, since it blends along the
d
th axis to the identity, using itself as the blend. Thus, we have

\begin{gathered}\varphi_{r+1}\sim\varphi_{r+1}^{\prime}\sim P_{NW}^{-1}\circ\varphi_{r+1}^{\prime}.\end{gathered}

(239)

By construction, this last
r
-blend fixes
\bar{\psi}_{0}
everywhere, and is this equivalent to the identity. Finally, we have

\begin{gathered}\varphi_{r+1}\sim 1,\end{gathered}

(240)

which is what we wanted to prove for injectivity.

Finally, the long exact sequence is the usual cofiber long exact sequence, and its interpretation comes from the construction of the map from
r
-blends to FDQC-invertible states.

∎

8
FDQC Representations from Group Cohomology

Given a class
[\omega]\in H^{d+2}(BG,U(1))
, for finite
G
, we will construct a QCA (actually FDQC)
G
-representation on a lattice composed of on-site Hilbert spaces
\mathbb{C}[G]
. This follows a well-known construction in
8
, reinterpreted in
37
. We will show it has a series of blends allowing a computation of the Else-Nayak index yielding precisely
[\omega]
.

This calculation works because beyond the first blend
\beta_{g}
(see
Section
4.2
),
\beta_{g,h}
and higher are phase operators and all commute. This makes it a calculation in ordinary group cohomology, not involving the higher non-abelian structure of
{\mathcal{Q}}({\mathcal{A}})
. (See the discussion in
Section
2.4
.)

To proceed with the construction, we must first recall some details of nonabelian cohomology/finite gauge theory
62
. Let
X
be a simplicial complex with ordered vertices. We define
G
-valued 1-cocycle
A\in Z^{1}(X,G)
to be an assignment of a group element

\begin{gathered}A(xy)\in G\end{gathered}

(241)

for each directed edge
x<y
, such that for each directed triangle
x<y<z
,

\begin{gathered}A(xy)A(yz)=A(xz).\end{gathered}

(242)

Given an “inhomogeneous” cocycle

\begin{gathered}\omega:G^{d+2}\to{\mathbb{R}}/2\pi{\mathbb{Z}}\end{gathered}

(243)

for group cohomology
5
;
32
and a
G
-valued 1-cocycle
A
, we obtain a
d+2
-cocycle

\begin{gathered}\omega(A)\in C^{d+2}(X,{\mathbb{R}}/2\pi{\mathbb{Z}})\\
\int_{\Delta}\omega(A):=\omega(A(x_{0}x_{1}),A(x_{1}x_{2}),\ldots,A(x_{d}x_{d+2})\end{gathered}

(244)

where
\Delta
is any
d+2
-simplex in
X
, with ordered vertices
x_{0}<x_{1}<\ldots<x_{d+2}
.

We also define a
G
-valued 0-cochain
f\in C^{0}(X,G)
to be an assignment
f_{x}\in G
for each vertex
x
.
C^{0}(X,G)
forms a group and acts on
Z^{1}(X,G)
by

\begin{gathered}A\mapsto A^{f}\\
A^{f}(xy)=f(x)A(xy)f(y)^{-1}.\end{gathered}

(245)

We think of this as a gauge transformation of
A
.

Consider two simplices
\Delta^{n}
and
\Delta^{m}
of dimension
n
and
m
respectively, with ordered vertices. We define first a triangulation of the product
\Delta^{n}\times\Delta^{m}
. Vertices of
\Delta^{n}\times\Delta^{m}
are given by pairs of vertices
(x,y)
,
x
a vertex of
\Delta^{n}
and
y
a vertex of
\Delta^{m}
. We give these the lexicographic ordering, meaning
(x_{1},y_{1})<(x_{2},y_{2})
if either
x_{1}<x_{2}
or
x_{1}=x_{2}
and
y_{1}<y_{2}
. The
k
-simplices of
\Delta^{n}\times\Delta^{m}
are given by ordered length
k+1
sequences of these vertices.

Figure 15:
The triangulation of
\Delta^{1}\times\Delta^{1}
and corresponding
A^{(f)}
.

Now suppose we are given
A\in Z^{1}(\Delta^{n},G)
and
\vec{f}=(f_{1},\ldots,f_{m})\in C^{0}(\Delta^{n},G)
. We will define a particular element

\begin{gathered}A^{\vec{f}}\in Z^{1}(\Delta^{n}\times\Delta^{m},G)\end{gathered}

(246)

such that
A^{\vec{f}}
restricts to
A^{f_{j}}
on
\Delta^{n}\times j
. In particular, for
m=1
, we get a cocycle
A^{(f)}
on
\Delta^{n}\times[0,1]
which on
\Delta^{n}\times 0
restricts to
A
and which on
\Delta^{n}\times 1
restricts to
A^{f}
. The construction for
n=m=1
is shown in Fig.
15
.

A^{\vec{f}}
is easiest to define in terms of paths. Suppose we are given two vertices
(x_{1},y_{1})<(x_{2},y_{2})
which is associated to an edge
E
. Let
y_{0}
be the initial vertex of
\Delta^{m}
. There is a unique longest ordered path
p_{1}
from
(x_{1},y_{0})
to
(x_{1},y_{1})
, increasing only the first coordinate one step at a time. Likewise there is a shortest path
p_{2}
from
(x_{1},y_{1})
to
(x_{2},y_{1})
and also
p_{3}
from
(x_{2},y_{1})
to
(x_{2},y_{2})
. Let
p_{1}
consist of the edges

\begin{gathered}(x_{1},y_{0})=(x_{1},z_{1})<(x_{1},z_{2})<\cdots<(x_{1},z_{p})=(x_{1},y_{1}).\end{gathered}

(247)

We define

\begin{gathered}\vec{f}(p_{1})=f_{z_{p-1}}(x_{1})\cdots f_{z_{2}}(x_{1})f_{z_{1}}(x_{1}),\end{gathered}

(248)

where we have identified the vertices
z_{i}
of
\Delta^{m}
with
1<2<\cdots<m+1
. Likewise let
p_{3}
consist of the edges

\begin{gathered}(x_{2},y_{1})=(x_{2},w_{1})<(x_{2},w_{2})<\cdots<(x_{2},w_{q})=(x_{2},y_{2})\end{gathered}

(249)

and define

\begin{gathered}\vec{f}(p_{3})=f_{w_{q-1}}(x_{1})\cdots f_{w_{2}}(x_{1})f_{w_{1}}(x_{1}).\end{gathered}

(250)

Let
p_{2}
consist of edges
e_{1},\ldots,e_{l}
. We also define

\begin{gathered}A(p_{1})=A(e_{l})A(e_{l-1})\cdots A(e_{1})\in G.\end{gathered}

(251)

Finally let

\begin{gathered}A^{\vec{f}}(E)=\vec{f}(p_{3})A(p_{2})\vec{f}(p_{1})^{-1}.\end{gathered}

(252)

It is not too hard to check that
A^{\vec{f}}
satisfies the cocycle equation (
242
) as well as the restriction conditions we wanted.

Now let
A\in Z^{1}(X,G)
. We define, for each
m
, the
m
th descendant

\begin{gathered}\omega_{m}(A,f_{1},\ldots,f_{m})\in C^{d+2-m}(X,{\mathbb{R}}/2\pi{\mathbb{Z}})\end{gathered}

(253)

where
f_{1},\ldots,f_{m}\in C^{0}(X,G)
, as follows. Take any
\Delta^{d+2-m}\subset X
and restrict
A
to it. We define

\begin{gathered}\int_{\Delta^{d+2-m}}\omega_{m}(A,f_{1},\ldots,f_{m}):=\int_{\Delta^{d+2-m}\times\Delta^{m}}\omega(A^{\vec{f}}).\end{gathered}

(254)

Note that for
m=d+2
,
X=\star
, the
d+2
nd descendant is equal to the inhomogeneous cocycle
\omega
evaluated on
f_{1},\ldots,f_{d+2}\in G
.

These descendants are extremely useful. For example, the 1st descendant satisfies

\begin{gathered}\omega(A^{f})-\omega(A)+d\omega_{1}(A,f)=0.\end{gathered}

(255)

This can be proven by considering
A^{(f)}
on
\Delta^{d+2}\times[0,1]
. Since
d\omega=0
, we have

\begin{gathered}0=\int_{\Delta^{d+2}\times[0,1]}d\omega(A^{(f)})=\int_{\partial(\Delta^{d+2}\times[0,1])}\omega(A^{(f)})\\
=\int_{\Delta^{d+2}\times 1}\omega(A^{f})-\int_{\Delta^{d+2}\times 0}\omega(A)+\int_{(\partial\Delta^{d+2})\times[0,1]}\omega(A^{(f)}\\
=\int_{\Delta^{d+2}\times 1}\omega(A^{f})-\int_{\Delta^{d+2}\times 0}\omega(A)+\int_{\partial\Delta^{d+2}}\omega_{1}(A,f).\end{gathered}

(256)

Likewise, the 2nd descendant satisfies

\begin{gathered}\omega_{1}(A,f_{2}f_{1})-\omega_{1}(A,f_{1})-\omega_{1}(A^{f_{1}},f_{2})+d\omega_{2}(A,f_{1},f_{2})=0\end{gathered}

(257)

which can be proven the same way. In general,
\omega_{m}
measures the boundary term needed to make
\omega_{m-1}
an
m-1
-cocycle for the group law on
C^{0}(X,G)
.

To warm up with a physics application, consider a
d+1
-manifold
X
with Hilbert space
\mathbb{C}[G]
associated to each vertex. We can define on it the wavefunction

\begin{gathered}|\omega_{1}\rangle=\sum_{f\in C^{0}(X,G)}e^{i\int_{X}\omega_{1}(0,f)}|f\rangle,\end{gathered}

(258)

where 0-cochains
f\in C^{0}(X,G)
label the product state group basis. Suppose we act on this Hilbert space by left multiplication by some
g\in G
. From the 2nd descendant we have the identity

\begin{gathered}\omega_{1}(0,gf)-\omega_{1}(0,f)+d\omega_{2}(0,f,g)=0\end{gathered}

(259)

(note
\omega_{1}(0,g)=0
, which can easily be proven from the definition). This implies that
|\omega_{1}\rangle
is symmetric up to a boundary term given by the 2nd descendant. This boundary term will give us our FDQC
G
-representation.

Let
Y^{d}
be a triangulated
d
-manifold with a Hilbert space
\mathbb{C}[G]
assigned to each vertex. For example, we could take
Y^{d}={\mathbb{R}}^{d}
with a triangulation whose vertices live on the integer lattice
{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}
. Then we would be in the usual setting of QCA
G
-representations studied in this work. However, the construction generally works as long as
Y^{d}
has no boundary. As before, we work in a basis labeled by 0-cochains
f\in C^{0}(Y,G)
. We define the following operator in this basis

\begin{gathered}U_{g}|f\rangle=e^{i\int_{Y}\omega_{2}(0,f,g)}|g\cdot f\rangle.\end{gathered}

(260)

This can be expressed as a finite depth quantum circuit, with one layer given by on-site left-multiplication
L_{g}
and the other layers defined by the mutually commuting local phase operators

\begin{gathered}|f\rangle\mapsto e^{i\int_{\Delta^{d}}\omega_{2}(0,f,g)}|f\rangle\end{gathered}

(261)

on each
d
-simplex
\Delta^{d}
. The circuit is finite depth because each vertex only belongs to a finite set of
d
-simplices, and
\int_{\Delta^{d}}\omega_{2}(0,f,g)
only depends on
f
restricted to
\Delta^{d}
.

The 3rd descendant
\omega_{3}(A,f,g_{1},g_{2})
satisfies

\begin{gathered}d\omega_{3}(A,f,g_{1},g_{2})=\omega_{2}(A,f,g_{1}g_{2})-\omega_{2}(A,f,g_{1})-\omega_{2}(A,g_{1}\cdot f,g_{2}).\end{gathered}

(262)

The existence of this descendant implies that
U_{g}
satisfies the
G
group law

\begin{gathered}U_{g_{1}}U_{g_{2}}=U_{g_{1}g_{2}}\end{gathered}

(263)

up to boundary terms. Thus these define an FDQC
G
-representation if
Y
has no boundary.

We can define a very convenient set of blends by taking our symmetry to act only on a triangulated submanifold
W\subset Y
with the same definition as above, applying left multiplication to vertices in
W
and integrating over
d
-simplices in
W
. Then we will have

\begin{gathered}(U^{W}_{gh})^{-1}U^{W}_{g}U^{W}_{h}|f\rangle=e^{i\int_{\partial W}\omega_{3}(0,f,g,h)}|f\rangle.\end{gathered}

(264)

We can then define a blend-of-blend to be

\begin{gathered}V^{\Gamma}_{g,h}|f\rangle=e^{i\int_{\Gamma}\omega_{3}(0,f,g,h)}|f\rangle,\end{gathered}

(265)

where
\Gamma
is any codimension 1 triangulated submanifold of
Y
. Proceeding in this way, we get a series of blends which we can always continue using the descendants. Note that they are all diagonal in the
C^{0}(Y,G)
basis. At the last stage, we find the local operator

\begin{gathered}N^{x}_{g_{1},\ldots,g_{d+1}}|f\rangle=e^{i\int_{x}\omega_{d+2}(0,f,g_{1},\ldots,g_{d+1})}|f\rangle=e^{i\omega(f(x),g_{1},\ldots,g_{d+1})}|f\rangle.\end{gathered}

(266)

We then ask if this operator satisfies a (twisted)
d+1
-cocycle equation for
g_{1},\ldots,g_{d+1}
. The Else-Nayak index
16
is

\begin{gathered}(N^{x}_{g_{1},\ldots,g_{d+1}})^{\pm}(N^{x}_{g_{1},\ldots,g_{d},g_{d+1}g_{d+2}})^{\mp}\cdots\\
\cdots(N^{x}_{g_{1}g_{2},g_{3},\ldots,g_{d+2}})^{-1}{\rm Ad}_{U_{g_{1}}}(N^{x}_{g_{2},\ldots,g_{d+2}}),\end{gathered}

(267)

where
\pm=(-1)^{d+1}
. This yields a pure phase operator whose exponent is

\begin{gathered}\pm\omega(f(x),g_{1},\ldots,g_{d+1})\mp\omega(f(x),g_{1},\ldots,g_{d+1}g_{d+2})+\cdots\\
\ +\omega(f(x)g_{1},g_{2},\ldots,g_{d+2}).\end{gathered}

(268)

This is equal to

\begin{gathered}(d\omega)(f(x),g_{1},\ldots,g_{d+2})+\omega(g_{1},\ldots,g_{d+2}).\end{gathered}

(269)

The first term is zero because
\omega
is a
d+2
-cocycle. Thus, the Else-Nayak index we obtain from these blends is precisely the cocycle we began with!

9
Dependence of Anomaly Indices on Choices of Lifts

In this appendix we consider stable anomalies for simplicity. We will show that the anomaly indices, including the Else-Nayak index, in general depend on the choices of lifts. We will prove the following proposition as well as provide some physical interpretation and an example.

Proposition 18
.

Suppose we have two lifts
\alpha_{k}
and
\tilde{\alpha}_{k}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Q}}^{d,k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{k+1}\pi_{k+1}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces BG}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c^{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{k},\tilde{\alpha}^{k}}

(270)

then the
k+1
st anomaly indices we compute between them differ by

\begin{gathered}-[c^{k}\circ\alpha^{k}]=[c^{k}\circ i^{k}\circ\beta^{k}],\end{gathered}

(271)

where
\beta^{k}:BG\to{\mathcal{Q}}_{k}^{d}
is a map into the fiber of the Whitehead map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Q}}^{d,k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{k}}

(272)

and measures the “difference” between the lifts, satisfying

\begin{gathered}-[\alpha^{k}]=[i^{k}\circ\beta^{k}]\end{gathered}

(273)

in the abelian group of homotopy classes of maps
BG\to{\mathcal{Q}}^{d,k}
.

These fibers
{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{k}
form the Postnikov tower for
\Omega_{\star}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}
:

\begin{gathered}\Omega_{\star}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}={\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{d+2}\to\cdots\to{\mathcal{Q}}_{k}^{d}\to{\mathcal{Q}}_{k-1}^{d}\to\cdots\to{\mathcal{Q}}_{1}^{d}=\pi_{1}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}\to{\mathcal{Q}}_{0}^{d}=\star\end{gathered}

(274)

and

\begin{gathered}\in H^{k+1}({\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{k},\pi_{k+1}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d})\end{gathered}

(275)

is the Postnikov invariant.

For lifts
\alpha^{k}
,
\tilde{\alpha}^{k}
which agree at the
k-1
level:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{k-1}\pi_{k}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Q}}^{d,k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{k+1}\pi_{k+1}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d,k-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces BG}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Q}}^{d,k-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c^{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{k},\tilde{\alpha}^{k}}

(276)

their difference is measured by
\gamma^{k}:BG\to B^{k-1}\pi_{k}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}
, and their
k+1
st anomalies indices satisfy

\begin{gathered}-[c^{k}\circ\alpha^{k}]=[c^{k}\circ j^{k}\circ\gamma^{k}],\end{gathered}

(277)

where

\begin{gathered}\in H^{k+1}(B^{k-1}\pi_{k}{\mathcal{Q}},\pi_{k+1}{\mathcal{Q}})\end{gathered}

(278)

is a “mini Postnikov invariant” classifying the “mini tower”
{\mathcal{Q}}^{d,k}\to{\mathcal{Q}}^{d,k-1}
.

In particular, the first and second anomaly indices are independent of choices, since
\alpha^{0}
is determined for us and

\begin{gathered}\in H^{2}(\pi_{1}{\mathcal{Q}},\pi_{2}{\mathcal{Q}})=0\end{gathered}

(279)

since
\pi_{1}{\mathcal{Q}}
is a discrete space.

Proof.

Let us recall the Whitehead tower of
{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}

\begin{gathered}B^{d+2}U(1)_{disc}={\mathcal{Q}}^{d,d+2}\to\cdots\to{\mathcal{Q}}^{d,2}\xrightarrow{p_{2}}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d,1}\xrightarrow{p_{1}}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d,0}={\mathcal{Q}}^{d}.\end{gathered}

(280)

Suppose that we have a stable
d
-QCA
G
-representation with corresponding map

\begin{gathered}\alpha:BG\to{\mathcal{Q}}^{d},\end{gathered}

(281)

and we are trying to determine its anomaly by taking lifts up the Whitehead tower. Suppose that we have constructed a lift up to step
k
, giving the following homotopy-commutative diagram (The square is a homotopy pullback square):

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{k}\pi_{k+1}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Q}}^{d,k+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\star}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Q}}^{d,k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{k+1}\pi_{k+1}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces BG}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c^{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{k}}

(282)

The
k+1
st anomaly index is the homotopy class of the map

\begin{gathered}c^{k}\circ\alpha^{k}:BG\to B^{k+1}\pi_{k+1}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}.\end{gathered}

(283)

We want to understand the dependence of this anomaly index on the choice of lift
\alpha^{k}
for fixed
\alpha
.

Suppose we choose a different lift
\tilde{\alpha}^{k}
. Since
p^{k}\circ\alpha^{k}=p^{k}\circ\tilde{\alpha}^{k}
up to homotopy, we can compare them fiberwise, obtaining a (homotopy class of) map

\begin{gathered}\beta^{k}:BG\to{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{k}\end{gathered}

(284)

where
{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{k}
is the fiber of
p^{k}
. In fact, since we are in the stable case,
{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}
is an infinite loop space, and so are each of the spaces
{\mathcal{Q}}^{d,k}
in the Whitehead tower. Maps
BG\to{\mathcal{Q}}^{d,k}
thus form an abelian group up to homotopy, and we in fact have

\begin{gathered}-[\alpha^{k}]=[i^{k}\circ\beta^{k}],\end{gathered}

(285)

where brackets denote homotopy class and
i^{k}:{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{k}\to{\mathcal{Q}}^{d,k}
is the inclusion of the fiber. To summarize, the situation is now

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{k}\pi_{k+1}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Q}}^{d,k+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\star}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Q}}^{d,k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{k+1}\pi_{k+1}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces BG}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c^{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta^{k}}

(286)

In terms of the anomaly indices, we have

\begin{gathered}-[c^{k}\circ\alpha_{k}]=[c^{k}\circ i^{k}\circ\beta^{k}]\in H^{k+1}(BG,\pi_{k+1}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}).\end{gathered}

(287)

Thus we see that the anomaly indices can depend on the lifts if

\begin{gathered}\in H^{k+1}({\mathcal{Q}}_{k}^{d},\pi_{k+1}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d})\end{gathered}

(288)

is nonzero.

The fibers
{\mathcal{Q}}_{k}^{d}
appearing in the Whitehead tower form their own tower called the “Postnikov tower”, in this case of
\Omega_{\star}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}

32
;
49
:

\begin{gathered}\Omega_{\star}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}={\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{d+2}\to\cdots\to{\mathcal{Q}}_{k}^{d}\to{\mathcal{Q}}_{k-1}^{d}\to\cdots\to{\mathcal{Q}}_{1}^{d}=\pi_{1}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}\to{\mathcal{Q}}_{0}^{d}=\star\end{gathered}

(289)

These form another sequence of fibrations (compare the Whitehead tower (
51
)):

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{k}\pi_{k+1}({\mathcal{Q}}^{d})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{k+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\star}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{k+1}\pi_{k+1}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c^{k}\circ i^{k}}

(290)

Note that the classifying map
c^{k}\circ i^{k}
, known as the Postnikov invariant, is the same combination appearing above.

Thus we find that in general,
nonzero Postnikov invariants can lead to dependence of the anomaly indices on choices of lifts
.

We can illustrate this more concretely in the case that
\alpha^{k}
and
\tilde{\alpha}^{k}
coincide all the way up to the
k-1
st level, meaning we have

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{k}\pi_{k+1}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Q}}^{d,k+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\star}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{k-1}\pi_{k}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Q}}^{d,k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{k+1}\pi_{k+1}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces BG}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Q}}^{d,k-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c^{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{k-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\alpha}^{k}}

(291)

Now the difference between
\alpha^{k}
and
\tilde{\alpha}^{k}
is measured simply by

\begin{gathered}\gamma^{k}:BG\to B^{k-1}\pi_{k}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}\\
[\tilde{\alpha}^{k}]-[\alpha_{k}]=[j^{k}\circ\gamma^{k}].\end{gathered}

(292)

This gives a difference in the
k+1
st anomaly index

\begin{gathered},\end{gathered}

(293)

where the “mini Postnikov class”

\begin{gathered}\in H^{k+1}(B^{k-1}\pi_{k}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d},\pi_{k+1}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d})\end{gathered}

(294)

appears, which classifies this two-piece subtower of the whole Postnikov tower of
\Omega_{\star}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}
. Note these are what appear in the
E^{2}
page of the Atiyah-Hirzebruch spectral sequence.
∎

Although the dependence of the anomaly indices on the choices of lifts may seem worrisome, it is actually a necessary feature for the spectrum of QCA to have. Recall that there is a map

\begin{gathered}B^{d+2}U(1)_{disc}\to{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}.\end{gathered}

(295)

This gives a map (for finite
G
the topology on
U(1)
doesn’t matter)

\begin{gathered}i:H^{d+2}(BG,U(1))\to{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}(BG)\end{gathered}

(296)

which can be understood as the inclusion of “group cohomology anomalies” in the group of stable blend anomalies. We can define this map in lattice terms by taking the FDQC
G
symmetries that come from group cohomology classes (see Appendix
8
), and measuring its stable blend anomaly.

We expect that the map
i
is neither injective nor surjective, and moreover that
H^{d+2}(BG,U(1))
can mix with a non-trivial extension of the quotient

\begin{gathered}{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}(BG)/i(H^{d+2}(BG,U(1))).\end{gathered}

(297)

Such phenomena are only possible if the Postnikov classes are non-vanishing (see eg.
63
and references therein).

A similar phenomenon is known in the study of the spectrum of invertible orientable TQFTs in
d+2
-dimensions
20
, which we denote
\Omega^{d+2}_{SO}
. There is likewise a map

\begin{gathered}H^{d+2}(BG,U(1))\to\Omega^{d+2}_{SO}(BG)\end{gathered}

(298)

which is known to have these properties
39
. This map can be considered as taking the orientable invertible TQFT defined by the Dijkgraaf-Witten path integral
13
.

We can also consider systems of fermions, and a spin TQFT spectrum
\Omega^{d+2}_{Spin}
, giving also a map

\begin{gathered}H^{d+2}(BG,U(1))\to\Omega^{d+2}_{Spin}(BG)\end{gathered}

(299)

corresponding again to Dijkgraaf-Witten theory but now considered as a spin TQFT. This has some low dimensional examples where group cohomology SPTs become trivial. We expect that the corresponding boundary theory has an anomalous symmetry with an ill-defined Else-Nayak invariant, reflecting that the Else-Nayak invariant is not an invariant of the bulk SPT phase.

The simplest example is for
G={\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
, and corresponds to

\begin{gathered}\frac{1}{2}A^{5}\in H^{5}(B{\mathbb{Z}}_{2},U(1)).\end{gathered}

(300)

On spin 5-manifolds manifolds, this class is always trivial, since

\begin{gathered}A^{5}=Sq^{2}A^{3}.\end{gathered}

(301)

We calculated a Postnikov class for
{\mathcal{Q}}^{3}(B{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})
in
Section
5
and found the same
Sq^{2}
operation appear. So the Atiyah-Hirzebruch spectral sequence has a differential on the
E^{2}
page eating
A^{5}
, in other words
A^{5}
maps to zero in
{\mathcal{Q}}^{3}(B{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})
. Thus, we should expect that the Else-Nayak invariant is ambiguous by this
A^{5}
term when we compute it on a 3d lattice.
